    22	\title{\textbf{Plant roots and long-term aridity set the atmospheric hydrogen-scavenging capacity of
    23	hyperarid Rub' al-Khali soils}}
    24	\author{\todo{author list and affiliations}\\ \small King Abdullah University of Science and Technology}
    25	\date{}
    26	
    27	\begin{document}
    28	\maketitle
    29	\linenumbers
    30	
    31	\section*{Abstract}
    32	Bacteria in desert soils can persist on energy from atmospheric hydrogen and carbon monoxide, which
    33	they oxidise with high-affinity enzymes. Where this capacity is concentrated, and whether it follows
    34	plants, long-term aridity or recent rain, has been inferred mostly from single-time transects of
    35	cold deserts. We typed the hydrogenase and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunits in 550
    36	shotgun metagenomes from 61 locations across the hyperarid Rub' al-Khali, sampled on five
    37	expeditions over two and a half years, and quantified each enzyme group in reads as copies per genome
    38	equivalent. Root-adjacent soil carried 4.7-fold less capacity for the group 1l [NiFe]-hydrogenase than
    39	bulk soil at the same 5--10~cm depth, in 89\% of 107 paired site visits and in every expedition. This
    40	loss exceeded that of control hydrogenases and carbon monoxide dehydrogenases by 1.97 log$_2$ units,
    41	whereas the group 1h hydrogenase and form-I carbon monoxide dehydrogenase were retained. Root-adjacent
    42	communities were enriched in faster-growing genomes, and the 1l loss was smaller at greener sites,
    43	but these proxies of plant carbon explained a minor part of the loss: carrying group 1l predicted
    44	the depletion of a genus beside roots beyond its predicted growth rate and phylum. Across sites,
    45	group 1l capacity in bulk soil fell with long-term mean annual precipitation (Spearman
    46	$\rho=-0.51$), while change between expeditions at the same site was unrelated to antecedent
    47	precipitation. Hydrogen and carbon monoxide scavenging capacity are therefore set separately in
    48	desert soil: plants select against the group 1l hydrogenase, and the spatial pattern of long-term
    49	aridity is carried by lineage composition that persisted through the rainfall between expeditions.
    50	
    51	% ============================================================================
    52	\section*{Introduction}
    53	Soil bacteria oxidise hydrogen and carbon monoxide at their trace atmospheric concentrations, and
    54	soil uptake is the largest sink of tropospheric hydrogen \citep{ehhalt2009h2cycle}. The oxidation
    55	is catalysed by enzymes with high substrate affinity: hydrogen by the group 1h and group 1l
    56	[NiFe]-hydrogenases \citep{greening2016genomic,ortiz2021antarctic,grinter2023structural} and carbon
    57	monoxide by the form-I carbon monoxide dehydrogenase \citep{cordero2019atmospheric}. Genomes
    58	encoding these enzymes are widespread in soil \citep{bay2021trace}, and the energy they yield
    59	sustains dormant cells \citep{greening2015persistence,cordero2019atmospheric}. In deserts, where
    60	photosynthetic input is sparse, atmospheric oxidation can also support primary production
    61	\citep{ji2017atmospheric,ray2022atmospheric}. The amount of this capacity in a soil therefore bears
    62	both on how desert communities persist and on the size of the soil hydrogen sink, to which arid
    63	regions contribute disproportionately \citep{reji2025h2sink}. What sets that amount within a desert
    64	is less clear.
    65	
    66	Three factors have been proposed, each from a different kind of study. Aridity gradients sampled
    67	once in space show that trace-gas oxidation gains weight as conditions dry: along a gradient in the
    68	Negev Desert, cell-specific rates of atmospheric hydrogen consumption increased 143-fold towards the
    69	hyperarid end together with the abundance of high-affinity hydrogenases \citep{bay2021aridity}, and
    70	in the Namib, hydrogen oxidation per 16S rRNA gene copy was highest in the most arid, fog-fed zone
    71	\citep{tribbia2026namib}. These transects compare places, and they treat spatial differences in
    72	aridity as a stand-in for change over time at one place, an assumption that sampling at a single
    73	time cannot test. Water addition experiments test moisture directly: hydrating an Australian desert
    74	soil in microcosms enriched cyanobacteria and decreased both the abundance and the expression of
    75	the group 1l hydrogenase associated with Actinobacteriota \citep{jordaan2020gobi}. These experiments
    76	follow one soil in the laboratory for weeks. Finally, plants concentrate organic carbon, and in a
    77	semiarid sandy desert the genes for hydrogen and carbon monoxide oxidation were less abundant under
    78	shrubs than in open soil and decreased as soil organic carbon increased, with the oxidisers behaving
    79	as oligotrophs whose uptake was suppressed by added carbon \citep{li2023fertile}. That study compared
    80	soil under and between shrubs over several depths and treated hydrogen oxidation as a single
    81	function, so it could not tell whether plants act on both high-affinity hydrogenase groups or on one
    82	of them. Most genome-resolved surveys of the high-affinity guild come from cold deserts
    83	\citep{ortiz2021antarctic,ray2022atmospheric,hutchinson2025antarctic}, and in situ data from hot
    84	deserts are among the gaps identified in a recent review \citep{ray2023mmbr}.
    85	
    86	We addressed these limitations in the Rub' al-Khali, a hyperarid sand desert in which long-term
    87	mean annual precipitation at our sites ranges from 14 to 48~mm. We sampled 60 transect sites on five
    88	expeditions between March 2023 and October 2025, each time at three soil positions: the surface, bulk
    89	soil at 5--10~cm and root-adjacent soil at the same 5--10~cm depth. Repeated visits allow spatial and
    90	temporal responses to precipitation to be compared directly, and the depth-matched root contrast
    91	separates the effect of plants from that of depth. To resolve the enzyme groups, we typed every
    92	hydrogenase and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunit in a catalogue of 5{,}438 species-level
    93	genomes and quantified each group in the reads of 550 metagenomes as copies per genome equivalent,
    94	with non-high-affinity hydrogenases and carbon monoxide dehydrogenases as internal controls. With
    95	this design we tested three hypotheses: that root-adjacent soil selectively loses the group 1l
    96	hydrogen scavengers (H1); that hydrogen and carbon monoxide scavenging capacity respond separately
    97	to soil position (H2); and that long-term aridity sets the spatial level of group 1l capacity while
    98	change between expeditions at a site is independent of antecedent precipitation (H3). Because soil
    99	organic carbon was not measured, we then tested two proxies for the plant-carbon mechanism proposed
   100	by \citet{li2023fertile}: vegetation greenness and the predicted growth rate of the community.
   101	
   102	The analysis code, the compact inputs and outputs and the figure code are available at
   103	\url{https://github.com/bio-ontology-research-group/empty-quarter-mags} in the folder
   104	\texttt{lifeonair/}.
   105	
   106	% ============================================================================
   107	\section*{Materials and methods}
   108	
   109	\subsection*{Sampling, sequencing and genome catalogue}
   110	Sampling, DNA extraction, sequencing and the genome catalogue are shared with companion papers on
   111	the same transect\todo{cite the data descriptor and the MAG catalogue paper}, and we summarise them
   112	here. Sixty sites along a 1{,}043~km west to east transect through the Saudi Rub' al-Khali were
   113	sampled on five expeditions (17--20 March 2023, 14 July 2023, 17--21 February 2024, 19--21 August
   114	2024 and 12--15 October 2025). At each site and visit, three co-located replicates were taken at
   115	three positions: surface soil (0~cm), bulk subsurface soil (5--10~cm) and soil from the root zone of
   116	the nearest plant at 5--10~cm, which we call root-adjacent because soil was not consistently
   117	attached to roots. Soil was frozen at $-20^\circ$C in the field and stored at $-80^\circ$C. DNA was
   118	extracted from 0.9--1.2~g of soil with the QIAGEN PowerLyzer PowerSoil or DNeasy PowerSoil Pro kit
   119	and sequenced on an Illumina NovaSeq 6000 (150-nt paired reads; 101-nt reads for the fourth
   120	expedition). Reads were quality-filtered with fastp v1.0.1 \citep{chen2018fastp}.
   121	
   122	Each sample was assembled with MEGAHIT v1.2.9 \citep{li2015megahit} and binned with SemiBin2
   123	v2.2.1 \citep{pan2023semibin2}; bins were refined with DAS\_Tool v1.1.7 \citep{sieber2018dastool},
   124	assessed with CheckM2 v1.1.0 \citep{chklovski2023checkm2}, and the 12{,}434 bins of at least medium
   125	quality (completeness $\geq 50\%$, contamination $\leq 10\%$) were dereplicated with dRep v3.6.2
   126	\citep{olm2017drep} at 95\% average nucleotide identity into 5{,}438 species representatives.
   127	Representatives were classified with GTDB-Tk v2.7.2 \citep{chaumeil2022gtdbtk} against GTDB release
   128	232 \citep{parks2022gtdb} and annotated with Bakta v1.12.0 \citep{schwengers2021bakta} and
   129	eggNOG-mapper v2.1.13 \citep{cantalapiedra2021eggnog}. Relative abundance of each representative
   130	per sample was computed with CoverM v0.7.0 \citep{aroney2025coverm} in genome mode.
   131	
   132	\subsection*{Typing of hydrogenase and carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunits}
   133	Proteins annotated with the [NiFe]-hydrogenase large subunit orthologue (KEGG K06281) and the
   134	carbon monoxide dehydrogenase large subunit orthologue (K03520) were extracted from the
   135	representatives. Each hydrogenase was assigned the class of its best DIAMOND blastp hit
   136	(\texttt{--ultra-sensitive}) in HydDB \citep{sondergaard2016hyddb,greening2016genomic}, using the
   137	sequences and classifier script of the Greening laboratory HydDB repository; groups 1h and 1l are
   138	the high-affinity hydrogen scavengers. Each CoxL protein was called form I at more than 55\%
   139	identity and at least 90\% query coverage to the three form-I references of
   140	\citet{cordero2019atmospheric} or when it carried the form-I active-site motif AYRGAGR; proteins
   141	meeting neither criterion are unresolved between forms. A representative carries a capacity when
   142	it encodes at least one protein of that class. Five representatives that refinement emitted as
   143	contig subsets lost their typed marker with the removed contigs and were dropped from the carrier
   144	lists.
   145	
   146	To check that typed genes sit in intact loci, we took the ten genes on each side of every typed
   147	large subunit on the same contig from the Bakta annotation and scored the presence of the
   148	hydrogenase small subunit (K06282, K05927, K00437), hydrogenase maturation genes (K04651--K04656,
   149	K03605, K03192, or Bakta product names) and, for CoxL, the medium and small subunits (K03519,
   150	K03518) and the accessory genes \textit{coxDEFG}. Proportions are reported for loci with at least
   151	five genes on both sides.
   152	
   153	For display, hydrogenase large subunits of at least 400 residues were clustered at 70\% identity
   154	with MMseqs2 v18.8cc5c \citep{steinegger2017mmseqs2}, combined with all HydDB group 1 and group 2
   155	references, aligned with MAFFT v7.526 \citep{katoh2013mafft} (\texttt{--auto}), stripped of columns
   156	with more than 50\% gaps and used to build a tree with FastTree v2.2.0 \citep{price2010fasttree}
   157	(\texttt{-lg -gamma}). The tree was rooted on the clade that best separates the group 2 references.
   158	CoxL proteins of at least 600 residues were treated in the same way with the form-I references.
   159	
   160	\subsection*{Read-based quantification}
   161	Capacity was quantified directly in reads so that sequences outside the genome catalogue
   162	contribute. A competitive protein database contained every typed hydrogenase large subunit of the
   163	representatives labelled with its HydDB class, all HydDB reference hydrogenases labelled with their
   164	group, every CoxL protein of the representatives labelled form I or other, the three form-I CoxL
   165	references, and 14 universal single-copy ribosomal proteins of the representatives (KEGG K02886,
   166	K02906, K02926, K02931, K02933, K02867, K02874, K02878, K02982, K02988, K02994, K02946, K02948 and
   167	K02965) clustered at 90\% identity. Because every read is assigned to its single best hit, related
   168	enzymes of other classes compete with the high-affinity ones for each read.
   169	
   170	From each sample, 10 million forward reads were drawn with seqtk v1.5 \citep{li2023seqtk} (seed 11)
   171	and truncated to 100~nt so that the 101-nt and 150-nt libraries were screened identically; reads
   172	shorter than 75~nt were discarded. Reads were searched with DIAMOND v2.0.15
   173	\citep{buchfink2021diamond} blastx (\texttt{--id 50 --evalue 1e-5 --max-target-seqs 1}), and each
   174	read's single best hit with an alignment of at least 25 residues was counted at identity floors of
   175	50, 60 and 70\%. For each sample and label, reads were divided by the length of the hit subject in
   176	kilobases and summed, and each marker was divided by the median of the 14 ribosomal-protein values.
   177	The result, genome equivalents (GE), is the number of marker copies per genome. GE measure relative
   178	capacity: a genome carries on average about 1.9 group 1l and 1.1 group 1h copies, so GE overestimate
   179	the fraction of group 1l carriers. The primary measure used the 60\% identity floor and samples in
   180	which at least 10 of the 14 ribosomal proteins were detected.
   181	
   182	The five measured markers are group 1l hydrogenase, group 1h hydrogenase, form-I CoxL, all other
   183	[NiFe]-hydrogenases (controls) and all other CoxL (controls). The CoxL control includes the
   184	unresolved proteins, some of which may be form I. Of the 573 sequenced samples screened, 550 had
   185	complete metadata and passed the ribosomal-protein filter (117 surface, 165 bulk and 268
   186	root-adjacent samples from 61 locations; median 9.77 million reads screened).
   187	
   188	\subsection*{Contamination screen}
   189	Two screens were applied to the representatives. The first flagged representatives whose GTDB
   190	genus matched the genus of a 16S rRNA amplicon variant called as a contaminant in 17 extraction
   191	blanks of the fifth expedition. The second re-implemented the frequency method of decontam
   192	\citep{davis2018decontam}, comparing a model in which abundance is independent of extracted DNA
   193	concentration with one in which it is inversely proportional, among the samples with a single
   194	recorded concentration in which the representative was present (flagged at a score below 0.1 when
   195	present in at least 10 samples). Flagged representatives were removed in a sensitivity analysis by
   196	discarding reads whose best hit was one of their proteins.
   197	
   198	\subsection*{Environmental covariates}
   199	Long-term mean annual precipitation per site was computed from daily ERA5 reanalysis precipitation
   200	\citep{hersbach2020era5} at 0.25$^\circ$ resolution for 1995--2024, obtained through the Open-Meteo
   201	archive, and independently from the NASA POWER 2001--2020 climatology of corrected precipitation
   202	based on MERRA-2 \citep{gelaro2017merra2}. Antecedent precipitation was the ERA5 sum over the 90 and
   203	365 days before the first sampling date of each site visit. Vegetation greenness was the median
   204	MODIS normalised difference vegetation index (NDVI; MOD13Q1 and MYD13Q1 v061, 250~m, 16-day
   205	composites \citep{didan2021mod13q1}) over the 60 days before sampling at the site coordinates.
   206	Soil organic carbon was not measured on these samples.
   207	
   208	\subsection*{Statistical analysis}
   209	Analyses were run in R v4.4.3 with lme4 v2.0.6 \citep{bates2015lme4}, vegan v2.7.5
   210	\citep{oksanen2025vegan} and ape v5.8.1 \citep{paradis2019ape}. Log-ratios used a pseudocount of
   211	0.01~GE. A site visit is one site sampled on one expedition; for paired contrasts between positions,
   212	each marker was summarised as the median over replicates within a site visit, and the log$_2$ ratio
   213	of the medians was computed for each visit with both positions. Confidence intervals were obtained by
   214	resampling sites with replacement (2{,}000 resamples), and the test is a Wilcoxon signed-rank test on
   215	site means of the visit values, so that the site is the independent unit.
   216	
   217	For H1, the excess loss of a high-affinity marker in a visit is its log$_2$ ratio between
   218	root-adjacent and bulk soil minus the mean of the two control log$_2$ ratios. Because markers share
   219	the ribosomal-protein denominator, a change in that denominator shifts all markers equally and
   220	cancels in the excess. A mixed model of log$_2$ GE in root-adjacent and bulk samples, with marker,
   221	position, their interaction and expedition as fixed effects and site, site visit and sample as
   222	random intercepts, tested the interaction by a likelihood-ratio test against the additive model.
   223	Order-level abundance of group 1l carriers was computed from CoverM abundances as a percentage of
   224	the mapped community, and the visit-level log$_2$ ratio was computed for the ten orders with the
   225	highest mean share in bulk soil.
   226	
   227	For H2, the primary test used the ratio of raw reads assigned to high-affinity hydrogenases (groups
   228	1h and 1l) to raw reads assigned to form-I CoxL, which has no shared normaliser. The share of
   229	variance explained by position was estimated for each marker from mixed models of log$_2$ GE with
   230	position and expedition as fixed effects and site as a random intercept, and tested by
   231	likelihood ratio. The effective number of carrier genera per sample was the exponential of the
   232	Shannon diversity of genus-level CoverM abundance among carriers.
   233	
   234	For H3, the spatial test correlated site medians of bulk-soil GE with long-term mean annual
   235	precipitation (Spearman). It was repeated with the second precipitation product, with a bootstrap
   236	over six spatial blocks defined by $k$-means clustering of site coordinates, with each block left
   237	out in turn, and as a partial rank correlation controlling for latitude, longitude and elevation
   238	and, separately, for the first principal coordinate of bulk-soil community composition (Bray--Curtis
   239	dissimilarity of site-mean CoverM profiles). A mixed model over all samples regressed log$_2$ GE on
   240	standardised log mean annual precipitation with position and expedition as fixed effects and site as
   241	a random intercept. For the temporal test, visit medians and log antecedent precipitation were
   242	centred within each site sampled at least twice, so that only change between expeditions at a site
   243	remained; the temporal slope was compared with the spatial slope of site means on log mean annual
   244	precipitation, with confidence intervals from 2{,}000 site resamples. Because antecedent
   245	precipitation varies mainly between expeditions, the temporal association was also tested in mixed
   246	models of log antecedent precipitation with and without expedition as a fixed effect.
   247	
   248	Turnover of composition was partitioned by PERMANOVA \citep{anderson2001permanova} on Bray--Curtis
   249	dissimilarities of CoverM profiles with expedition, site and position as marginal terms (999
   250	permutations). Synchrony of carrier genera across expeditions at the 37 sites sampled at least four
   251	times was measured with the $\phi$ statistic of \citet{loreau2008synchrony} against a null from
   252	permuting expedition labels within genus at each site.
   253	
   254	Sensitivity variants recomputed H1--H3 at identity floors of 50 and 70\%, without reads to
   255	contamination-flagged representatives, without reads to the 122 group 1l proteins typed below 50\%
   256	identity or 80\% coverage to their HydDB hit, without the fifth expedition, and without the fourth
   257	expedition (101-nt libraries).
   258	
   259	\subsection*{Proxies for the plant-carbon mechanism}
   260	For vegetation input, log$_2$ group 1l GE in each position was modelled on standardised NDVI and
   261	standardised log mean annual precipitation with site and expedition as random intercepts, and the
   262	visit-level excess loss of group 1l was related to NDVI by Spearman correlation of site medians and
   263	by a mixed model with site and expedition as random intercepts.
   264	
   265	For life strategy, the minimum doubling time of each representative was predicted from codon usage
   266	bias with gRodon2 v2.7.3 \citep{weissman2021grodon} in partial mode, using coding sequences of at
   267	least 240~nt from the Bakta annotation and the ribosomal-protein genes as the highly expressed set;
   268	estimates required at least ten ribosomal-protein genes. Doubling time was compared between carriers
   269	and non-carriers of each capacity in a mixed model of log doubling time with carrier indicators as
   270	fixed effects and order as a random intercept. The community-weighted mean log doubling time of a
   271	sample was the CoverM-abundance-weighted mean over representatives with an estimate, and its shift
   272	between root-adjacent and bulk soil was computed per site visit as above. The visit-level excess
   273	loss of group 1l was regressed on this shift in a mixed model with site and expedition as random
   274	intercepts. Finally, for the 288 genera with a mean share of at least 0.05\% of the mapped community
   275	in bulk soil, the median visit-level log$_2$ ratio between root-adjacent and bulk soil was regressed
   276	on the median log doubling time of the genus and the fractions of its representatives carrying
   277	group 1l, group 1h and form-I CoxL, with phylum as a fixed effect and weights equal to the square
   278	root of the bulk-soil share.
   279	
   280	% ============================================================================
   281	\section*{Results}
   282	
   283	\subsection*{Group 1l dominates hydrogen-scavenging capacity in Rub' al-Khali soil}
   284	We first established which high-affinity enzymes the Rub' al-Khali community encodes. Of the 5{,}438
   285	species representatives, 968 encode a high-affinity hydrogenase (597 group 1l, 410 group 1h, 39
   286	both), 1{,}170 a form-I CoxL and 1{,}860 at least one of them. The typed group 1l and 1h proteins
   287	form separate clades among the HydDB references (Fig.~\ref{fig:h123}a). They sit in hydrogenase
   288	loci: among loci with at least five genes on each side, a small-subunit gene is adjacent in 73\% of
   289	group 1l and 84\% of group 1h loci, and maturation genes are within ten genes in 88\% and 96\%; 73\%
   290	of form-I CoxL loci contain the complete \textit{coxMSL} operon. Group 1l proteins outnumber group 1h
   291	proteins 1{,}124 to 445.
   292	
   293	The same predominance holds in reads. In bulk soil, the median sample carried 1.08 group 1l copies
   294	per genome equivalent, 0.14 group 1h copies and 0.62 form-I CoxL copies. Group 1l is therefore the
   295	principal hydrogen-scavenging capacity, as in Antarctic and Gobi desert soils
   296	\citep{ortiz2021antarctic,jordaan2020gobi}, and carbon monoxide scavenging capacity is of comparable
   297	magnitude. With this baseline, the position contrasts below ask where in the soil each capacity is
   298	held.
   299	
   300	\subsection*{Root-adjacent soil selectively loses group 1l capacity}
   301	If plants select against hydrogen scavengers, root-adjacent soil should carry less high-affinity
   302	hydrogenase than bulk soil at the same depth, and the deficit should exceed any general change in
   303	gene content. We compared root-adjacent and bulk soil in the 107 site visits at 48 sites where both
   304	were sampled at 5--10~cm. Group 1l GE were lower beside roots by a median of 2.23 log$_2$ units, a
   305	4.7-fold difference (95\% CI $-2.82$ to $-1.57$; lower in 89\% of visits; $p=8\times10^{-9}$;
   306	Fig.~\ref{fig:h123}b). The control hydrogenases and CoxL were lower by 0.33 and 0.38 units, so the
   307	excess loss of group 1l beyond the controls was 1.97 units ($-2.53$ to $-1.05$;
   308	$p=3\times10^{-8}$). Group 1h was depleted no more than the controls ($-0.06$; $p=0.90$), and form-I CoxL was
   309	slightly better retained than the controls ($+0.17$, 0.08 to 0.23). In the mixed model, the
   310	interaction of marker and position was strong ($\chi^2=308$, 4 df, $p=2\times10^{-65}$), and the only
   311	interaction term that departed from the reference was that of group 1l ($-1.74$, SE 0.12).
   312	
   313	The loss was consistent across expeditions and lineages. Group 1l GE were lower beside roots in each
   314	of the five expeditions (median log$_2$ ratios $-1.6$ to $-3.4$), and all ten orders of group 1l
   315	carriers with the highest bulk-soil abundance were depleted, including Acidimicrobiales ($-2.8$),
   316	Solirubrobacterales ($-1.7$), Rubrobacterales ($-2.3$) and Gaiellales ($-2.4$)
   317	(Table~\ref{tab:lineages}). The excess loss was between $-1.61$ and $-1.97$ in every sensitivity
   318	variant: at 50 and 70\% identity, without contamination-flagged representatives, with weak group 1l
   319	calls removed, and without either the fifth or the fourth expedition (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity}).
   320	Root-adjacent soil therefore selectively loses the group 1l hydrogen scavengers, as H1 predicted,
   321	while it retains group 1h and form-I CoxL capacity.
   322	
   323	\subsection*{Plant-carbon proxies explain a minor part of the group 1l loss}
   324	\citet{li2023fertile} attributed the depletion of trace-gas oxidisers under desert shrubs to organic
   325	carbon, which favours fast-growing copiotrophs over oligotrophic scavengers
   326	\citep{fierer2007copiotroph}. Without organic-carbon measurements, we tested two consequences of
   327	this mechanism. If plant input depresses group 1l, capacity should be lower where vegetation is
   328	greener, and the root contrast should shrink where bulk soil already receives plant input. Both held.
   329	Group 1l GE fell with NDVI in bulk soil ($-0.64$ log$_2$ units per standard deviation, SE 0.16) and
   330	at the surface ($-1.23$, SE 0.15), with mean annual precipitation in the model, with which site NDVI
   331	correlates ($\rho=0.57$); in root-adjacent soil, group 1l GE were unrelated to NDVI ($-0.02$). The
   332	excess loss beside roots was smaller at greener sites ($\rho=0.36$, $p=0.014$, 47 sites;
   333	Fig.~\ref{fig:mechanism}a).
   334	
   335	If organic carbon favours copiotrophs, root-adjacent communities should be faster-growing, and the
   336	group 1l loss should scale with that shift. We predicted minimum doubling times for 4{,}817
   337	representatives. Within orders, carriers of group 1l had longer doubling times than non-carriers
   338	(log doubling time $+0.12$, SE 0.03; medians 4.2 and 3.3~h), but so did carriers of group 1h
   339	($+0.11$, SE 0.03) and, to a lesser degree, form-I CoxL ($+0.06$, SE 0.02). The representatives
   340	with an estimate accounted for a median 98\% of mapped abundance. Root-adjacent communities were
   341	faster-growing than bulk soil in 79\% of 106 visits (community log doubling time $-0.25$;
   342	$p=1.5\times10^{-5}$; Fig.~\ref{fig:mechanism}b), and the group 1l excess loss was larger where this
   343	shift was larger ($\rho=0.22$, $p=0.023$; slope 1.55 log$_2$ units per unit log doubling time, SE
   344	0.62). The model, however, predicts an excess loss of 1.59 units in a visit with no shift
   345	(Fig.~\ref{fig:mechanism}c), close to the observed median of 1.97.
   346	
   347	Because carriers of both hydrogenase groups grow slowly, a shift to faster growers would deplete
   348	group 1h as well as group 1l. We therefore asked, across genera, whether carrying group 1l predicts depletion beside
   349	roots once growth rate is accounted for. Among 288 genera, slow-growing genera were more depleted
   350	(slope $-0.37$ per unit log doubling time, $p=6\times10^{-4}$), and the fraction of a genus carrying
   351	group 1l predicted additional depletion ($-0.75$ log$_2$ units, $p=3\times10^{-5}$) with phylum in
   352	the model; the fractions carrying group 1h ($+0.07$) and form-I CoxL ($-0.02$) were unrelated to
   353	depletion (Fig.~\ref{fig:mechanism}d). Plant input and the associated shift to faster growers contribute to
   354	the root-adjacent loss, but most of the loss is specific to group 1l and remains after growth rate
   355	and phylum are accounted for.
   356	
   357	\subsection*{Hydrogen and carbon monoxide scavenging capacity respond separately to soil position}
   358	The root contrast already shows hydrogen capacity falling while carbon monoxide capacity is
   359	retained. H2 tests whether this separation is general across positions and robust to the shared
   360	denominator. The raw-read ratio of high-affinity hydrogenase to form-I CoxL, which uses no
   361	normaliser, fell by 1.60 log$_2$ units from bulk to root-adjacent soil ($-1.94$ to $-1.15$) and by
   362	0.95 units from bulk to surface soil ($-1.45$ to $-0.48$). At the surface, group 1l GE were 1.03
   363	units and group 1h 0.62 units lower than in bulk soil, whereas form-I CoxL was unchanged ($-0.02$,
   364	$-0.09$ to 0.05; $p=0.13$; Fig.~\ref{fig:h123}c). Position explained 20.5\% of the variance in group
   365	1l GE, 5.5\% for group 1h, 3.1\% for form-I CoxL and 6--7\% for the controls.
   366	
   367	The two capacities also differ in how many lineages hold them. In the median sample, carbon monoxide
   368	capacity was spread over about 20 effective genera and high-affinity hydrogen capacity over 10 to 11,
   369	in every position. Carbon monoxide scavenging capacity is therefore held by more lineages and varies
   370	little with soil position, while hydrogen scavenging capacity, concentrated in group 1l carriers,
   371	tracks position, as H2 predicted.
   372	
   373	\subsection*{Long-term aridity sets the spatial level of group 1l capacity, while change over time is independent of recent rain}
   374	Position explains how capacity is distributed within a site. Between sites, H3 predicted that
   375	long-term aridity sets the level of group 1l capacity. Site medians of group 1l GE in bulk soil
   376	decreased with long-term mean annual precipitation ($\rho=-0.51$, $p=1.2\times10^{-4}$, 51 sites;
   377	Fig.~\ref{fig:h123}d), and with the independent NASA POWER product ($\rho=-0.43$, $p=0.0015$). The
   378	association held in a bootstrap over spatial blocks ($-0.81$ to $-0.18$), with each block left out
   379	($-0.61$ to $-0.37$), and after controlling for latitude, longitude and elevation ($-0.48$,
   380	$p=4\times10^{-4}$). Across all samples, group 1l GE fell by 0.49 log$_2$ units per standard
   381	deviation of log mean annual precipitation ($p=4\times10^{-5}$), group 1h by 0.14 ($p=0.09$) and
   382	form-I CoxL by 0.04 ($p=0.41$). The driest sites carry the most group 1l capacity, in line with the
   383	increase of high-affinity hydrogenases towards the dry end of other desert gradients
   384	\citep{bay2021aridity,tribbia2026namib}.
   385	
   386	Aridity also structures the community as a whole. The first principal coordinate of bulk-soil
   387	composition covaries with mean annual precipitation, and group 1l carriers form a large part of that
   388	axis; controlling for it reduced the correlation of group 1l GE with precipitation to $\rho=-0.20$
   389	($p=0.16$). The spatial association of group 1l capacity with aridity therefore operates through the
   390	composition of lineages, which changes along the aridity gradient.
   391	
   392	If spatial differences in aridity stand for change over time, group 1l capacity at a site should
   393	rise after dry years and fall after wet ones. Within sites, change in bulk-soil group 1l GE between
   394	expeditions was unrelated to the precipitation of the preceding year ($\rho=0.02$, $p=0.89$; 71
   395	visits at 27 sites). The confidence interval of the temporal slope ($-1.57$ to $1.70$ log$_2$ units
   396	per unit log precipitation) excludes the spatial slope ($-4.15$). At the surface, group 1l GE rose
   397	after wet years ($\rho=0.38$, $p=0.0017$), but antecedent precipitation differed mainly between
   398	expeditions, and with expedition in the model the association weakened to $p=0.31$.
   399	Form-I CoxL at the surface retained an association with antecedent precipitation after adjustment
   400	for expedition ($p=0.010$).
   401	
   402	The temporal result fits the stability of the communities over the 31 months of sampling.
   403	Expedition explained 2.0\% of the variation in community composition, compared with 29.4\% for site
   404	and 4.6\% for position, and the same partition held among carriers alone (2.0\%, 32.6\% and 3.7\%).
   405	Carrier genera fluctuated in step (median synchrony $\phi=0.115$ against 0.048 under the null at 37
   406	sites), so the stability of capacity reflects the persistence of the lineages that carry it. The spatial pattern therefore records long-term aridity
   407	through lineage composition, which persisted through the rainfall between our expeditions, and
   408	space-for-time substitution fails for group 1l capacity in bulk soil, as H3 predicted.
   409	
   410	% ============================================================================
   411	\section*{Discussion}
   412	Plant roots and long-term aridity set the hydrogen-scavenging capacity of Rub' al-Khali soil through
   413	the group 1l [NiFe]-hydrogenase. Root-adjacent soil carried about a fifth of the group 1l capacity of bulk
   414	soil at the same depth while retaining group 1h and form-I CoxL capacity, and between sites, group 1l
   415	capacity was highest where long-term precipitation was lowest. Carbon monoxide scavenging capacity
   416	varied little with either factor and was held by about twice as many genera.
   417	
   418	The closest precedent is the loss of trace-gas oxidisers under shrubs in the Mu Us Desert
   419	\citep{li2023fertile}. Our results agree on the direction and on the involvement of plant carbon:
   420	greener sites carried less group 1l capacity, and root-adjacent communities were faster-growing.
   421	They add three findings. First, the loss is specific to one of the two high-affinity hydrogenase
   422	groups; resolving group 1l from group 1h shows that the plant effect falls on group 1l, even though
   423	carriers of both are slow-growing. Second, the loss is measured beyond control genes that share the same normaliser, which separates
   424	selection on the capacity from general changes in gene content. Third, plant-carbon proxies account for a minor part of the loss, and carriage
   425	of group 1l predicts depletion of a genus beyond its growth rate and phylum. A factor that
   426	distinguishes the group 1l carriers is therefore required. Moisture is the most direct candidate if
   427	root-adjacent soil is wetter than open sand: hydration of an Australian desert soil decreased the abundance and
   428	expression of group 1l while group 1h expression remained stable \citep{jordaan2020gobi}. Roots also
   429	alter the oxygen and hydrogen regime of the surrounding soil, and differences between the two enzymes
   430	in their tolerance of these conditions could favour group 1h beside roots. Both explanations remain to
   431	be tested.
   432	
   433	The separation of hydrogen from carbon monoxide capacity has a counterpart in Li et al., who found a
   434	weaker decline of carbon monoxide than of hydrogen oxidation genes under shrubs \citep{li2023fertile}.
   435	In the Rub' al-Khali, form-I CoxL capacity was retained beside roots and at the surface, and its
   436	distribution over more genera matches the functional redundancy reported for carbon monoxide
   437	dehydrogenase in southern African drylands \citep{saumasanchez2024redundancy}. A broad carrier base
   438	may buffer carbon monoxide scavenging capacity against the turnover of individual lineages, whereas
   439	hydrogen scavenging capacity, concentrated in fewer group 1l lineages, follows their distribution.
   440	
   441	The spatial association with aridity extends to a hot sand desert the pattern of other gradients, in
   442	which high-affinity hydrogenases and cell-specific hydrogen oxidation increase towards the arid end
   443	\citep{bay2021aridity,tribbia2026namib}. Repeated sampling adds a test over time. Change between
   444	expeditions at a site was unrelated to the preceding year's rainfall, and the spatial pattern
   445	operated through lineage composition, which varied about 15 times more between sites than between expeditions.
   446	Group 1l capacity therefore integrates aridity over periods longer than our 31 months of sampling.
   447	For models of the soil hydrogen sink, which weight arid regions heavily \citep{reji2025h2sink}, this
   448	implies that long-term aridity and vegetation cover predict where scavenging capacity is held, and
   449	that interannual rainfall variation changes it little.
   450	
   451	The design measures genomic capacity and its distribution. Capacity predicts uptake in some soils \citep{reji2025h2sink}, but activity depends on moisture and
   452	temperature \citep{jordaan2020gobi,hutchinson2025antarctic}, and the next step is to measure
   453	hydrogen and carbon monoxide uptake in archived root-adjacent and bulk soil from the same visits.
   454	Tribbia et al. measured uptake in 2~g of soil stored at 4$^\circ$C \citep{tribbia2026namib}, and
   455	potential rates in our frozen soil would test whether the root-adjacent loss of group 1l capacity
   456	reduces hydrogen uptake. Extracellular DNA can persist in soil \citep{carini2016relicdna,lennon2018relicdna},
   457	and only nine propidium-monoazide-treated pairs are available for this transect, so part of the
   458	measured capacity may be relic DNA, which treating the soil with propidium monoazide before
   459	extraction would remove. In the absence of soil organic carbon and moisture measurements, NDVI and
   460	predicted growth rate stand in for the plant-carbon mechanism; measurements of
   461	organic carbon and water content in the root-adjacent and bulk soil would test it directly. Finally,
   462	precipitation is taken from reanalysis at 0.25$^\circ$, and the five expeditions sample a limited
   463	range of antecedent rainfall, so a response to rare large rain events remains possible.
   464	
   465	% ============================================================================
   466	\section*{Data and code availability}
   467	The shotgun reads are deposited at the European Nucleotide Archive under study PRJEB125153
   468	(public release scheduled for 1 November 2026)\todo{confirm release date and sample accessions}. The
   469	species representatives and their annotations are released with the companion catalogue paper. The
   470	analysis scripts, compact input and output tables, figure code and an R environment specification
   471	are at \url{https://github.com/bio-ontology-research-group/empty-quarter-mags} in the folder
   472	\texttt{lifeonair/}; per-sample read counts are retrieved by the repository's large-file script,
   473	which checks them against a checksum manifest\todo{archived release DOI before submission}.

exec
/bin/bash -lc "sed -n '227,322p' lifeonair/manuscript/main.tex; nl -ba lifeonair/code/h123_analysis.R" in /home/leechuck/Documents/papers/empty-quarter-mags
 succeeded in 0ms:
For H2, the primary test used the ratio of raw reads assigned to high-affinity hydrogenases (groups
1h and 1l) to raw reads assigned to form-I CoxL, which has no shared normaliser. The share of
variance explained by position was estimated for each marker from mixed models of log$_2$ GE with
position and expedition as fixed effects and site as a random intercept, and tested by
likelihood ratio. The effective number of carrier genera per sample was the exponential of the
Shannon diversity of genus-level CoverM abundance among carriers.

For H3, the spatial test correlated site medians of bulk-soil GE with long-term mean annual
precipitation (Spearman). It was repeated with the second precipitation product, with a bootstrap
over six spatial blocks defined by $k$-means clustering of site coordinates, with each block left
out in turn, and as a partial rank correlation controlling for latitude, longitude and elevation
and, separately, for the first principal coordinate of bulk-soil community composition (Bray--Curtis
dissimilarity of site-mean CoverM profiles). A mixed model over all samples regressed log$_2$ GE on
standardised log mean annual precipitation with position and expedition as fixed effects and site as
a random intercept. For the temporal test, visit medians and log antecedent precipitation were
centred within each site sampled at least twice, so that only change between expeditions at a site
remained; the temporal slope was compared with the spatial slope of site means on log mean annual
precipitation, with confidence intervals from 2{,}000 site resamples. Because antecedent
precipitation varies mainly between expeditions, the temporal association was also tested in mixed
models of log antecedent precipitation with and without expedition as a fixed effect.

Turnover of composition was partitioned by PERMANOVA \citep{anderson2001permanova} on Bray--Curtis
dissimilarities of CoverM profiles with expedition, site and position as marginal terms (999
permutations). Synchrony of carrier genera across expeditions at the 37 sites sampled at least four
times was measured with the $\phi$ statistic of \citet{loreau2008synchrony} against a null from
permuting expedition labels within genus at each site.

Sensitivity variants recomputed H1--H3 at identity floors of 50 and 70\%, without reads to
contamination-flagged representatives, without reads to the 122 group 1l proteins typed below 50\%
identity or 80\% coverage to their HydDB hit, without the fifth expedition, and without the fourth
expedition (101-nt libraries).

\subsection*{Proxies for the plant-carbon mechanism}
For vegetation input, log$_2$ group 1l GE in each position was modelled on standardised NDVI and
standardised log mean annual precipitation with site and expedition as random intercepts, and the
visit-level excess loss of group 1l was related to NDVI by Spearman correlation of site medians and
by a mixed model with site and expedition as random intercepts.

For life strategy, the minimum doubling time of each representative was predicted from codon usage
bias with gRodon2 v2.7.3 \citep{weissman2021grodon} in partial mode, using coding sequences of at
least 240~nt from the Bakta annotation and the ribosomal-protein genes as the highly expressed set;
estimates required at least ten ribosomal-protein genes. Doubling time was compared between carriers
and non-carriers of each capacity in a mixed model of log doubling time with carrier indicators as
fixed effects and order as a random intercept. The community-weighted mean log doubling time of a
sample was the CoverM-abundance-weighted mean over representatives with an estimate, and its shift
between root-adjacent and bulk soil was computed per site visit as above. The visit-level excess
loss of group 1l was regressed on this shift in a mixed model with site and expedition as random
intercepts. Finally, for the 288 genera with a mean share of at least 0.05\% of the mapped community
in bulk soil, the median visit-level log$_2$ ratio between root-adjacent and bulk soil was regressed
on the median log doubling time of the genus and the fractions of its representatives carrying
group 1l, group 1h and form-I CoxL, with phylum as a fixed effect and weights equal to the square
root of the bulk-soil share.

% ============================================================================
\section*{Results}

\subsection*{Group 1l dominates hydrogen-scavenging capacity in Rub' al-Khali soil}
We first established which high-affinity enzymes the Rub' al-Khali community encodes. Of the 5{,}438
species representatives, 968 encode a high-affinity hydrogenase (597 group 1l, 410 group 1h, 39
both), 1{,}170 a form-I CoxL and 1{,}860 at least one of them. The typed group 1l and 1h proteins
form separate clades among the HydDB references (Fig.~\ref{fig:h123}a). They sit in hydrogenase
loci: among loci with at least five genes on each side, a small-subunit gene is adjacent in 73\% of
group 1l and 84\% of group 1h loci, and maturation genes are within ten genes in 88\% and 96\%; 73\%
of form-I CoxL loci contain the complete \textit{coxMSL} operon. Group 1l proteins outnumber group 1h
proteins 1{,}124 to 445.

The same predominance holds in reads. In bulk soil, the median sample carried 1.08 group 1l copies
per genome equivalent, 0.14 group 1h copies and 0.62 form-I CoxL copies. Group 1l is therefore the
principal hydrogen-scavenging capacity, as in Antarctic and Gobi desert soils
\citep{ortiz2021antarctic,jordaan2020gobi}, and carbon monoxide scavenging capacity is of comparable
magnitude. With this baseline, the position contrasts below ask where in the soil each capacity is
held.

\subsection*{Root-adjacent soil selectively loses group 1l capacity}
If plants select against hydrogen scavengers, root-adjacent soil should carry less high-affinity
hydrogenase than bulk soil at the same depth, and the deficit should exceed any general change in
gene content. We compared root-adjacent and bulk soil in the 107 site visits at 48 sites where both
were sampled at 5--10~cm. Group 1l GE were lower beside roots by a median of 2.23 log$_2$ units, a
4.7-fold difference (95\% CI $-2.82$ to $-1.57$; lower in 89\% of visits; $p=8\times10^{-9}$;
Fig.~\ref{fig:h123}b). The control hydrogenases and CoxL were lower by 0.33 and 0.38 units, so the
excess loss of group 1l beyond the controls was 1.97 units ($-2.53$ to $-1.05$;
$p=3\times10^{-8}$). Group 1h was depleted no more than the controls ($-0.06$; $p=0.90$), and form-I CoxL was
slightly better retained than the controls ($+0.17$, 0.08 to 0.23). In the mixed model, the
interaction of marker and position was strong ($\chi^2=308$, 4 df, $p=2\times10^{-65}$), and the only
interaction term that departed from the reference was that of group 1l ($-1.74$, SE 0.12).

The loss was consistent across expeditions and lineages. Group 1l GE were lower beside roots in each
of the five expeditions (median log$_2$ ratios $-1.6$ to $-3.4$), and all ten orders of group 1l
carriers with the highest bulk-soil abundance were depleted, including Acidimicrobiales ($-2.8$),
Solirubrobacterales ($-1.7$), Rubrobacterales ($-2.3$) and Gaiellales ($-2.4$)
(Table~\ref{tab:lineages}). The excess loss was between $-1.61$ and $-1.97$ in every sensitivity
variant: at 50 and 70\% identity, without contamination-flagged representatives, with weak group 1l
calls removed, and without either the fifth or the fourth expedition (Table~\ref{tab:sensitivity}).
Root-adjacent soil therefore selectively loses the group 1l hydrogen scavengers, as H1 predicted,
while it retains group 1h and form-I CoxL capacity.

     1	#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
     2	# Life on air (P3): registered analyses for the three hypotheses (decided 9 October 2026).
     3	#
     4	#   H1  Root-adjacent soil selectively loses group 1l H2 scavengers. PlantRoot and Deep are both
     5	#       5-10 cm (data-paper methods), so PlantRoot/Deep is a same-depth root contrast. The 1l
     6	#       depletion must exceed the depletion of control markers (non-high-affinity [NiFe] large
     7	#       subunits and non-form-I CoxL) sharing the same SCG denominator.
     8	#   H2  H2 and CO scavenging capacity are decoupled across microhabitats: high-affinity hydB varies
     9	#       with compartment, form-I CoxL does not. Primary test uses the raw read ratio
    10	#       (1h + 1l reads) / (form-I coxL reads), which has no shared normaliser.
    11	#   H3  Long-term aridity (ERA5 1995-2024 and NASA POWER 2001-2020 mean annual precipitation) sets the
    12	#       spatial 1l baseline, while within-site, between-trip change does not track antecedent
    13	#       precipitation (space-for-time substitution fails).
    14	#
    15	# Primary measure: read-based genome equivalents (GE) at 60% identity (r3_aggregate.py). GE are
    16	# relative capacity (copies per genome equivalent), not cell fractions or activity.
    17	# Sensitivity variants: identity 50/70; reads to contamination-flagged reps removed; reads to group 1l
    18	# proteins typed below 50% identity or 80% coverage removed (strict_1l_calls); trip 5
    19	# excluded (high 1l median, possible batch); trip 4 excluded (101-nt libraries, truncated to 100 nt).
    20	#
    21	#   Rscript lifeonair/code/h123_analysis.R   (repository root; env lifeonair/code/environment.yml)
    22	
    23	suppressMessages({ library(lme4); library(vegan) })
    24	set.seed(20261009)
    25	IN  <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"; RD <- "lifeonair/results/reads"
    26	OUT <- "lifeonair/results/h123"; dir.create(OUT, showWarnings = FALSE, recursive = TRUE)
    27	rd  <- function(f, ...) read.delim(f, sep = "\t", quote = "", comment.char = "", stringsAsFactors = FALSE,
    28	                                   check.names = FALSE, ...)
    29	EPS <- 0.01
    30	NB  <- 2000
    31	MARKERS  <- c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI", "hydB_other_nife", "coxL_other")
    32	CONTROLS <- c("hydB_other_nife", "coxL_other")
    33	
    34	meta <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv"))
    35	meta <- meta[meta$trip != "" & meta$compartment != "", ]
    36	site_env <- rd(file.path(IN, "env_site.tsv"))
    37	site_env <- merge(site_env, rd(file.path(IN, "nasa_power_precip_climatology.tsv"))[, c("site", "map_power_mm")], by = "site")
    38	st_env <- rd(file.path(IN, "env_site_trip.tsv"))
    39	
    40	load_ge <- function(file, floor, drop_trips = character()) {
    41	  g <- rd(file.path(RD, file)); g <- g[g$id_floor == floor & g$scg_genes_detected >= 10, ]
    42	  d <- merge(meta, g, by.x = "sample_id", by.y = "sample")
    43	  d[!d$trip %in% drop_trips, ]
    44	}
    45	variants <- list(
    46	  primary_id60        = list(file = "guild_genome_equivalents.tsv", floor = 60, drop = character()),
    47	  id50                = list(file = "guild_genome_equivalents.tsv", floor = 50, drop = character()),
    48	  id70                = list(file = "guild_genome_equivalents.tsv", floor = 70, drop = character()),
    49	  no_flagged_reps     = list(file = "guild_genome_equivalents_noflagged.tsv", floor = 60, drop = character()),
    50	  strict_1l_calls     = list(file = "guild_genome_equivalents_strict1l.tsv", floor = 60, drop = character()),
    51	  without_trip5       = list(file = "guild_genome_equivalents.tsv", floor = 60, drop = "trip5"),
    52	  without_trip4       = list(file = "guild_genome_equivalents.tsv", floor = 60, drop = "trip4"))
    53	
    54	# cell = site x trip; per-cell compartment medians of each marker
    55	cells <- function(d, a, b, vars) {
    56	  key <- paste(d$site, d$trip, sep = "|")
    57	  out <- NULL
    58	  for (k in unique(key)) {
    59	    x <- d[key == k & d$compartment == a, ]; y <- d[key == k & d$compartment == b, ]
    60	    if (!nrow(x) || !nrow(y)) next
    61	    row <- data.frame(site = x$site[1], trip = x$trip[1])
    62	    for (v in vars) row[[v]] <- log2((median(x[[v]]) + EPS) / (median(y[[v]]) + EPS))
    63	    out <- rbind(out, row)
    64	  }
    65	  out
    66	}
    67	site_boot <- function(df, f) {          # cluster bootstrap over sites
    68	  s <- unique(df$site)
    69	  replicate(NB, { b <- sample(s, replace = TRUE); f(do.call(rbind, lapply(b, function(z) df[df$site == z, ]))) })
    70	}
    71	ci <- function(x) sprintf("[%.2f, %.2f]", quantile(x, .025, na.rm = TRUE), quantile(x, .975, na.rm = TRUE))
    72	site_wilcox <- function(df, v) { s <- tapply(df[[v]], df$site, mean); wilcox.test(s)$p.value }
    73	
    74	# ================================================================== H1
    75	h1_core <- function(d) {
    76	  c1 <- cells(d, "PlantRoot", "Deep", MARKERS)
    77	  c1$excess_1l <- c1$hydB_1l - rowMeans(c1[, CONTROLS])
    78	  c1$excess_1h <- c1$hydB_1h - rowMeans(c1[, CONTROLS])
    79	  c1$excess_formI <- c1$coxL_formI - rowMeans(c1[, CONTROLS])
    80	  c1
    81	}
    82	c1 <- h1_core(load_ge(variants$primary_id60$file, 60))
    83	write.table(c1, file.path(OUT, "h1_cell_log2fc_plantroot_vs_deep.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    84	h1_tab <- do.call(rbind, lapply(c(MARKERS, "excess_1l", "excess_1h", "excess_formI"), function(v) {
    85	  b <- site_boot(c1, function(x) median(x[[v]]))
    86	  data.frame(quantity = v, n_cells = nrow(c1), n_sites = length(unique(c1$site)), median_log2fc = median(c1[[v]]),
    87	             boot95 = ci(b), frac_cells_below0 = mean(c1[[v]] < 0), site_level_wilcoxon_p = site_wilcox(c1, v))
    88	}))
    89	h1_trip <- aggregate(cbind(hydB_1l, excess_1l) ~ trip, data = c1, FUN = median)
    90	h1_trip$n_cells <- as.numeric(table(c1$trip)[h1_trip$trip])
    91	
    92	# marker x compartment mixed model (long format, PlantRoot + Deep)
    93	d0 <- load_ge(variants$primary_id60$file, 60)
    94	dl <- do.call(rbind, lapply(MARKERS, function(v) {
    95	  x <- d0[d0$compartment %in% c("PlantRoot", "Deep"), c("sample_id", "site", "trip", "compartment")]
    96	  x$marker <- v; x$y <- log2(d0[[v]][d0$compartment %in% c("PlantRoot", "Deep")] + EPS); x }))
    97	dl$marker <- relevel(factor(dl$marker), ref = "hydB_other_nife")
    98	dl$compartment <- relevel(factor(dl$compartment), ref = "Deep")
    99	m_full <- lmer(y ~ marker * compartment + trip + (1 | site) + (1 | site:trip) + (1 | sample_id), data = dl, REML = FALSE)
   100	m_red  <- lmer(y ~ marker + compartment + trip + (1 | site) + (1 | site:trip) + (1 | sample_id), data = dl, REML = FALSE)
   101	a <- anova(m_red, m_full)
   102	fx <- summary(m_full)$coefficients
   103	h1_mm <- data.frame(term = rownames(fx)[grepl(":", rownames(fx))], fx[grepl(":", rownames(fx)), , drop = FALSE], row.names = NULL)
   104	h1_mm$interaction_LRT_chisq <- a$Chisq[2]; h1_mm$interaction_LRT_df <- a$Df[2]; h1_mm$interaction_LRT_p <- a$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2]
   105	write.table(h1_mm, file.path(OUT, "h1_marker_by_compartment_mixed_model.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   106	
   107	# lineages carrying the 1l signal (CoverM, PlantRoot vs Deep at order level)
   108	ab <- rd(gzfile(file.path(IN, "coverm_rep_abundance_long.tsv.gz")), header = FALSE, col.names = c("sample", "rep", "abund"))
   109	ab <- ab[ab$rep != "unmapped" & is.finite(ab$abund) & ab$abund > 0, ]
   110	cls <- rd(file.path(IN, "atmo_classification.tsv"))
   111	reps_1l <- unique(cls$rep_id[cls$marker == "hydB" & grepl("Group_1l$", cls$assignment)])
   112	tax <- rbind(rd(file.path(IN, "r232_bac120.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2], rd(file.path(IN, "r232_ar53.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2])
   113	rk  <- function(cl, p) ifelse(grepl(paste0(p, "__[^;]"), cl), sub(paste0(".*", p, "__([^;]*).*"), "\\1", cl), "unassigned")
   114	tax$phylum <- rk(tax$classification, "p"); tax$order <- rk(tax$classification, "o"); tax$genus <- rk(tax$classification, "g")
   115	a1 <- ab[ab$rep %in% reps_1l & ab$sample %in% d0$sample_id, ]
   116	a1$order <- paste(tax$phylum, tax$order, sep = ";")[match(a1$rep, tax$user_genome)]
   117	mapped <- tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)
   118	a1$pct <- 100 * a1$abund / as.numeric(mapped[a1$sample])
   119	om <- xtabs(pct ~ sample + order, data = a1); om <- as.data.frame.matrix(unclass(om))
   120	om$sample_id <- rownames(om); om <- merge(meta, om, by = "sample_id")
   121	ords <- setdiff(names(om), names(meta))
   122	top <- names(sort(colSums(om[om$compartment == "Deep", ords]), decreasing = TRUE))[1:10]
   123	c_ord <- cells(om, "PlantRoot", "Deep", top)
   124	h1_lin <- data.frame(order = top, deep_mean_pct_mapped = colMeans(om[om$compartment == "Deep", top]),
   125	                     median_log2fc_plantroot_vs_deep = apply(c_ord[, top], 2, median),
   126	                     frac_cells_below0 = apply(c_ord[, top], 2, function(v) mean(v < 0)), row.names = NULL)
   127	write.table(h1_lin, file.path(OUT, "h1_lineages_1l_carriers.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   128	
   129	# ================================================================== H2
   130	d0$lr_H2_CO <- log2((d0$reads_hydB_1l + d0$reads_hydB_1h + 0.5) / (d0$reads_coxL_formI + 0.5))
   131	d0$lr_1l_CO <- log2((d0$reads_hydB_1l + 0.5) / (d0$reads_coxL_formI + 0.5))
   132	h2_rows <- NULL
   133	for (pr in list(c("PlantRoot", "Deep"), c("Surface", "Deep"), c("PlantRoot", "Surface"))) {
   134	  cc <- cells(d0, pr[1], pr[2], c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI"))
   135	  # raw read ratio difference (log2 of ratio-of-ratios), no shared SCG normaliser
   136	  key <- paste(d0$site, d0$trip)
   137	  rr <- do.call(rbind, lapply(split(d0, key), function(x) {
   138	    a <- x[x$compartment == pr[1], ]; b <- x[x$compartment == pr[2], ]
   139	    if (!nrow(a) || !nrow(b)) return(NULL)
   140	    data.frame(site = a$site[1], trip = a$trip[1], d_lr_H2_CO = median(a$lr_H2_CO) - median(b$lr_H2_CO))
   141	  }))
   142	  for (v in c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI")) {
   143	    b <- site_boot(cc, function(x) median(x[[v]]))
   144	    h2_rows <- rbind(h2_rows, data.frame(contrast = paste(pr, collapse = "/"), quantity = paste0("log2FC_GE_", v),
   145	      n_cells = nrow(cc), median = median(cc[[v]]), boot95 = ci(b), site_level_wilcoxon_p = site_wilcox(cc, v)))
   146	  }
   147	  b <- site_boot(rr, function(x) median(x$d_lr_H2_CO))
   148	  h2_rows <- rbind(h2_rows, data.frame(contrast = paste(pr, collapse = "/"), quantity = "delta_log2_raw_read_ratio_H2HA_to_formI_CO",
   149	    n_cells = nrow(rr), median = median(rr$d_lr_H2_CO), boot95 = ci(b), site_level_wilcoxon_p = site_wilcox(rr, "d_lr_H2_CO")))
   150	}
   151	# per-marker compartment effect size in the same mixed-model frame
   152	h2_var <- do.call(rbind, lapply(c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI", CONTROLS), function(v) {
   153	  x <- d0; x$y <- log2(x[[v]] + EPS)
   154	  f1 <- lmer(y ~ compartment + trip + (1 | site), data = x, REML = FALSE)
   155	  f0 <- lmer(y ~ trip + (1 | site), data = x, REML = FALSE)
   156	  vf <- var(as.vector(model.matrix(f1)[, grep("^compartment", colnames(model.matrix(f1)))] %*%
   157	                      fixef(f1)[grep("^compartment", names(fixef(f1)))]))
   158	  vt <- vf + var(as.vector(model.matrix(f1)[, grep("^trip", colnames(model.matrix(f1)))] %*%
   159	                           fixef(f1)[grep("^trip", names(fixef(f1)))])) + sum(as.data.frame(VarCorr(f1))$vcov)
   160	  data.frame(marker = v, compartment_LRT_p = anova(f0, f1)$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2], compartment_var_share = vf / vt)
   161	}))
   162	# carrier redundancy: effective number of genera (exp Shannon) carrying each capacity per sample
   163	hyd_reps <- unique(cls$rep_id[cls$marker == "hydB" & grepl("Group_1[hl]$", cls$assignment)])
   164	cox_reps <- readLines(file.path(IN, "guild_coxL_reps.txt"))
   165	effn <- function(reps) {
   166	  x <- ab[ab$rep %in% reps & ab$sample %in% d0$sample_id, ]
   167	  x$genus <- tax$genus[match(x$rep, tax$user_genome)]
   168	  g <- tapply(x$abund, list(x$sample, x$genus), sum); g[is.na(g)] <- 0
   169	  setNames(exp(diversity(g, "shannon")), rownames(g))
   170	}
   171	eh <- effn(hyd_reps); ec <- effn(cox_reps)
   172	red <- merge(data.frame(sample_id = names(eh), eff_genera_H2 = eh), data.frame(sample_id = names(ec), eff_genera_CO = ec))
   173	red <- merge(meta[, c("sample_id", "compartment")], red)
   174	h2_red <- aggregate(cbind(eff_genera_H2, eff_genera_CO) ~ compartment, data = red, FUN = median)
   175	write.table(h2_rows, file.path(OUT, "h2_decoupling_contrasts.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   176	write.table(h2_var, file.path(OUT, "h2_compartment_effect_by_marker.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   177	write.table(h2_red, file.path(OUT, "h2_carrier_redundancy.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   178	
   179	# ================================================================== H3
   180	prank <- function(x, y, Z) {   # partial Spearman: correlate rank residuals after regressing on Z ranks
   181	  rx <- rank(x); ry <- rank(y); Zr <- apply(as.matrix(Z), 2, rank)
   182	  ex <- resid(lm(rx ~ Zr)); ey <- resid(lm(ry ~ Zr)); ct <- cor.test(ex, ey)
   183	  c(rho = unname(ct$estimate), p = ct$p.value)
   184	}
   185	site_env$block <- kmeans(scale(site_env[, c("lat", "lon")]), centers = 6, nstart = 50)$cluster
   186	# community-composition axis (Deep samples, site means; PCoA axis 1 of Bray-Curtis)
   187	deep_ids <- meta$sample_id[meta$compartment == "Deep"]
   188	cm <- xtabs(abund ~ sample + rep, data = ab[ab$sample %in% deep_ids, ]); cm <- unclass(cm); cm <- cm / rowSums(cm)
   189	cm_site <- rowsum(cm, meta$site[match(rownames(cm), meta$sample_id)]) /
   190	           as.numeric(table(meta$site[match(rownames(cm), meta$sample_id)])[rownames(rowsum(cm, meta$site[match(rownames(cm), meta$sample_id)]))])
   191	pc1 <- cmdscale(vegdist(cm_site, "bray"), k = 1)[, 1]
   192	site_env$comp_pc1_deep <- pc1[as.character(site_env$site)]
   193	
   194	h3_rows <- NULL; h3_lobo <- NULL
   195	for (comp in c("Deep", "Surface", "PlantRoot")) for (v in c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI")) {
   196	  x <- d0[d0$compartment == comp, ]
   197	  s <- aggregate(x[[v]], list(site = x$site), median); names(s)[2] <- "ge"
   198	  s <- merge(s, site_env, by = "site")
   199	  for (pv in c("map_era5_mm", "map_power_mm")) {
   200	    r0 <- cor.test(s$ge, s[[pv]], method = "spearman", exact = FALSE)
   201	    pg <- prank(s$ge, s[[pv]], s[, c("lat", "lon", "elevation_m")])
   202	    sc <- s[!is.na(s$comp_pc1_deep), ]
   203	    pc <- prank(sc$ge, sc[[pv]], sc[, c("comp_pc1_deep"), drop = FALSE])
   204	    bb <- replicate(NB, { b <- sample(unique(s$block), replace = TRUE)
   205	                          z <- do.call(rbind, lapply(b, function(k) s[s$block == k, ]))
   206	                          suppressWarnings(cor(z$ge, z[[pv]], method = "spearman")) })
   207	    lobo <- sapply(unique(s$block), function(k) { z <- s[s$block != k, ]; cor(z$ge, z[[pv]], method = "spearman") })
   208	    h3_rows <- rbind(h3_rows, data.frame(compartment = comp, marker = v, precip = pv, n_sites = nrow(s),
   209	      rho = unname(r0$estimate), p = r0$p.value, block_boot95 = ci(bb),
   210	      leave_one_block_out_range = sprintf("%.2f to %.2f", min(lobo), max(lobo)),
   211	      partial_rho_latlonelev = pg[["rho"]], partial_p_latlonelev = pg[["p"]],
   212	      partial_rho_compPC1 = pc[["rho"]], partial_p_compPC1 = pc[["p"]]))
   213	  }
   214	}
   215	# mixed model with log MAP (ERA5) across compartments
   216	d3 <- merge(d0, site_env[, c("site", "map_era5_mm", "map_power_mm")], by = "site")
   217	h3_mm <- do.call(rbind, lapply(c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI"), function(v) {
   218	  d3$y <- log2(d3[[v]] + EPS); d3$lmap <- as.numeric(scale(log(d3$map_era5_mm)))
   219	  f1 <- lmer(y ~ compartment + trip + lmap + (1 | site), data = d3, REML = FALSE)
   220	  f0 <- lmer(y ~ compartment + trip + (1 | site), data = d3, REML = FALSE)
   221	  data.frame(marker = v, beta_per_sd_logMAP = fixef(f1)[["lmap"]], LRT_p = anova(f0, f1)$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2])
   222	}))
   223	# temporal: within-site, between-trip change vs antecedent precipitation (ERA5 record ends 2024)
   224	h3_t <- NULL
   225	for (comp in c("Deep", "Surface", "PlantRoot")) for (v in c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI")) {
   226	  x <- d0[d0$compartment == comp, ]
   227	  cc <- aggregate(log2(x[[v]] + EPS), list(site = x$site, trip = x$trip), median); names(cc)[3] <- "y"
   228	  cc <- merge(cc, st_env, by = c("site", "trip"))
   229	  for (pv in c("P90_era5", "P365_era5")) {
   230	    z <- cc[!is.na(cc[[pv]]), ]; z$lp <- log(z[[pv]] + 1)
   231	    z <- z[z$site %in% names(which(table(z$site) >= 2)), ]
   232	    z$y_d  <- z$y  - ave(z$y,  z$site); z$lp_d <- z$lp - ave(z$lp, z$site)
   233	    tr <- cor.test(z$y_d, z$lp_d, method = "spearman", exact = FALSE)
   234	    slope_t <- coef(lm(y_d ~ lp_d, data = z))[2]
   235	    bt <- replicate(NB, { b <- sample(unique(z$site), replace = TRUE)
   236	                          w <- do.call(rbind, lapply(b, function(k) z[z$site == k, ])); coef(lm(y_d ~ lp_d, data = w))[2] })
   237	    sm <- aggregate(cbind(y, lp) ~ site, data = z, FUN = mean)
   238	    sm <- merge(sm, site_env[, c("site", "map_era5_mm")]); slope_s <- coef(lm(y ~ log(map_era5_mm), data = sm))[2]
   239	    h3_t <- rbind(h3_t, data.frame(compartment = comp, marker = v, antecedent = pv, n_cells = nrow(z),
   240	      n_sites = length(unique(z$site)), within_site_rho = unname(tr$estimate), within_site_p = tr$p.value,
   241	      temporal_slope = slope_t, temporal_slope_boot95 = ci(bt),
   242	      min_detectable_slope_80pct = 2.8 * sd(bt), spatial_slope_logMAP = slope_s))
   243	  }
   244	}
   245	# temporal, adjusted for trip (antecedent precipitation varies mostly between trips, so a
   246	# within-site slope can be aliased with trip / batch): y ~ log(P365 + 1) + trip + (1 | site)
   247	h3_tfe <- NULL
   248	for (comp in c("Deep", "Surface", "PlantRoot")) for (v in c("hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI")) {
   249	  x <- merge(d0[d0$compartment == comp, ], st_env, by = c("site", "trip"))
   250	  x <- x[!is.na(x$P365_era5), ]; x$y <- log2(x[[v]] + EPS); x$lp <- log(x$P365_era5 + 1)
   251	  f1 <- lmer(y ~ lp + trip + (1 | site), data = x, REML = FALSE); f0 <- lmer(y ~ trip + (1 | site), data = x, REML = FALSE)
   252	  g1 <- lmer(y ~ lp + (1 | site), data = x, REML = FALSE);        g0 <- lmer(y ~ (1 | site), data = x, REML = FALSE)
   253	  h3_tfe <- rbind(h3_tfe, data.frame(compartment = comp, marker = v, n_samples = nrow(x),
   254	    beta_logP365_no_trip = fixef(g1)[["lp"]], p_no_trip = anova(g0, g1)$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2],
   255	    beta_logP365_trip_adjusted = fixef(f1)[["lp"]], p_trip_adjusted = anova(f0, f1)$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2]))
   256	}
   257	write.table(h3_tfe, file.path(OUT, "h3_temporal_trip_adjusted.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   258	write.table(h3_rows, file.path(OUT, "h3_spatial_aridity.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   259	write.table(h3_mm, file.path(OUT, "h3_mixed_model_logMAP.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   260	write.table(h3_t, file.path(OUT, "h3_temporal_space_for_time.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   261	write.table(site_env, file.path(OUT, "h3_site_table.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   262	
   263	# ================================================================== sensitivity
   264	sens <- do.call(rbind, lapply(names(variants), function(nm) {
   265	  v <- variants[[nm]]; d <- load_ge(v$file, v$floor, v$drop)
   266	  c1v <- h1_core(d)
   267	  s <- aggregate(d$hydB_1l[d$compartment == "Deep"], list(site = d$site[d$compartment == "Deep"]), median)
   268	  s <- merge(s, site_env, by = "site")
   269	  dd <- d; dd$lr <- log2((dd$reads_hydB_1l + dd$reads_hydB_1h + 0.5) / (dd$reads_coxL_formI + 0.5))
   270	  rr <- cells(transform(dd, lr = lr + 0), "PlantRoot", "Deep", "lr")
   271	  data.frame(variant = nm, n_samples = nrow(d),
   272	             H1_1l_log2fc = median(c1v$hydB_1l), H1_excess_1l = median(c1v$excess_1l),
   273	             H1_site_p = site_wilcox(c1v, "excess_1l"),
   274	             H2_formI_log2fc = median(c1v$coxL_formI),
   275	             H3_deep_1l_rho_ERA5 = cor(s$x, s$map_era5_mm, method = "spearman"),
   276	             H3_deep_1l_rho_POWER = cor(s$x, s$map_power_mm, method = "spearman"))
   277	}))
   278	write.table(sens, file.path(OUT, "sensitivity.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   279	
   280	# ================================================================== summary
   281	sink(file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt"))
   282	cat("Life on air (P3) H1-H3, primary measure: read-based GE at 60% identity\n\n== H1 PlantRoot vs Deep (same 5-10 cm depth), per site x trip ==\n")
   283	print(h1_tab, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   284	cat("\nper trip:\n"); print(h1_trip, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   285	cat(sprintf("\nmarker x compartment interaction LRT: chisq %.1f, df %d, p %.2g\n", a$Chisq[2], a$Df[2], a$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2]))
   286	print(h1_mm[, 1:3], row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   287	cat("\n1l-carrier orders (CoverM, % of mapped):\n"); print(h1_lin, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   288	cat("\n== H2 decoupling ==\n"); print(h2_rows, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   289	cat("\n"); print(h2_var, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   290	cat("\neffective number of carrier genera (median per sample):\n"); print(h2_red, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   291	cat("\n== H3 spatial aridity (site medians) ==\n"); print(h3_rows, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   292	cat("\n"); print(h3_mm, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   293	cat("\n== H3 temporal (within-site, between-trip) ==\n"); print(h3_t, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   294	cat("\ntrip-adjusted temporal models:\n"); print(h3_tfe, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   295	cat("\n== sensitivity ==\n"); print(sens, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   296	cat("\n"); print(sessionInfo()$otherPkgs |> names())
   297	sink()

# Round 1 literature digest (Gemini 3.8 Flash via agy, 8 search directions, 12 Oct 2026)

Candidates were proposed by Flash and every DOI was checked against Crossref (title match); 5 mismatched DOIs were corrected by hand and none of the retained DOIs is fabricated. **The one-line relevance notes are Flash summaries and are NOT verified**: check the local PDF/XML before relying on any claim. Local files are in `lifeonair/literature/<key>.pdf` or `<key>.fulltext.xml`; "no local copy" means paywalled (citation + DOI only).

Search prompts: `search/round1/prompts/`; raw outputs with Flash syntheses: `search/round1/out/`.

## Cross-direction themes raised by Flash (to be checked, not established)

1. **Compositional artefact for H1**: relative (per-genome) profiling can show oligotroph "loss" when rhizosphere biomass expands (Azarbad 2022); growth rate is reported as a dominant predictor of the rhizosphere effect (Lopez et al. 2023 ISME J), which bears on the claim that growth rate explains only a minor part of the 1l loss.
2. **Group 1l physiology**: Imminger 2024 (Nat Commun) reportedly finds desert 1l expression responsive to hydration; Kropp 2026 (mSystems) catabolite repression of high-affinity hydrogenase by glycerol; Baril & Constant 2023 labile-carbon repression; de la Porte 2024 rhizosphere effect promoting gas-oxidation persistence (contrary direction).
3. **Space-for-time**: failure of substitution is predicted by theory when temporal variance is small relative to the spatial gradient (Blois 2013, Damgaard 2019, Perret 2024, Lovell 2023, Knapp 2026 aridity vs drought); legacy effects (Hawkes 2017, Canarini 2021). Risk that "space-for-time fails" is framed as more novel/strong than a 31-month design permits.
4. **H2 novelty**: broader taxonomic spread/redundancy of form-I CoxL than of high-affinity hydrogenases reported elsewhere (King 2007, Islam 2019, Liu 2023 Dalangtan, Latorre 2026, Wildbur 2026).
5. **Covariate quality**: ERA5 precipitation reportedly poor over hyperarid Saudi Arabia (Wang 2025, Nelli 2024).
6. **Capacity vs activity / relic DNA**: Buzzard 2022, Bertagni 2021, Horstmann 2024, Li 2026 eLife, Carini 2020, Blazewicz 2013, Rocca 2015; contamination reporting standards for low-biomass samples (Fierer 2025 Nat Microbiol).

   180	prank <- function(x, y, Z) {   # partial Spearman: correlate rank residuals after regressing on Z ranks
   181	  rx <- rank(x); ry <- rank(y); Zr <- apply(as.matrix(Z), 2, rank)
   182	  ex <- resid(lm(rx ~ Zr)); ey <- resid(lm(ry ~ Zr)); ct <- cor.test(ex, ey)
   183	  c(rho = unname(ct$estimate), p = ct$p.value)
   184	}

     2	# Life on air (P3), read-based guild abundance -- stage R1: build the competitive protein database.
     3	#
     4	# Database = labelled proteins; every read is assigned to its single best hit, so related but
     5	# non-atmospheric enzymes compete with the high-affinity ones:

    42	echo "[$(date -Iseconds)] extract SCG ribosomal proteins from the 5,438 reps"
    43	python3 - "$R" "$DB" <<'PY'
    44	import os, csv, glob, sys
    45	R, OUTD = sys.argv[1], sys.argv[2]
    46	TREE = "/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/EmptyQuarter_Data/processed/sequencing-results/sites"
    47	REPS = f"{R}/05_refine/_drep/out/dereplicated_genomes"
    48	SCG = {"K02886": "rplB", "K02906": "rplC", "K02926": "rplD", "K02931": "rplE", "K02933": "rplF",
    49	       "K02867": "rplK", "K02874": "rplN", "K02878": "rplP", "K02982": "rpsC", "K02988": "rpsE",
    50	       "K02994": "rpsH", "K02946": "rpsJ", "K02948": "rpsK", "K02965": "rpsS"}
    51	meta = {r["sample"]: r for r in csv.DictReader(open(f"{R}/metadata/sample_metadata.tsv"), delimiter="\t")}
    52	
    53	def resolve(rep, kind):                       # identical resolver to lifeonair_2_type_guild.sh
    54	    sample = rep.split("__")[0]
    55	    m = meta.get(sample)
    56	    if "__" not in rep or not m:
    57	        return None
    58	    rest = rep.split("__", 1)[1]
    59	    tok, local = rest.split("_", 1)
    60	    anno = "single_sample" if tok == "single" else "self_supervised"
    61	    base = f"{TREE}/{m['trip']}/site{m['site']}/{sample}/wgs/annotation/{anno}"
    62	    cands = [local] + ([local[:-4]] if local.endswith("_sub") else [])
    63	    for lc in cands:
    64	        p = f"{base}/eggnog/{lc}/{lc}.emapper.annotations" if kind == "eggnog" \
    65	            else f"{base}/bakta/{lc}/{lc}.faa"
    66	        if os.path.exists(p):
    67	            return p
    68	    return None
    69	
    70	def scg_proteins(ann_path):
    71	    hits, hdr = {}, None
    72	    with open(ann_path) as fh:
    73	        for line in fh:
    74	            if line.startswith("#query"):
    75	                hdr = line.rstrip("\n").lstrip("#").split("\t"); continue
    76	            if line.startswith("#") or not hdr:
    77	                continue
    78	            row = dict(zip(hdr, line.rstrip("\n").split("\t")))
    79	            ko = row.get("KEGG_ko", "-")
    80	            if not ko or ko == "-":
    81	                continue
    82	            ks = [k.replace("ko:", "") for k in ko.split(",")]
    83	            g = [SCG[k] for k in ks if k in SCG]
    84	            if len(g) == 1:
    85	                hits[row["query"]] = g[0]
    86	    return hits
    87	
    88	def read_faa(path):
    89	    name, seq = None, []
    90	    with open(path) as fh:
    91	        for line in fh:
    92	            if line.startswith(">"):
    93	                if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)
    94	                name = line[1:].split()[0]; seq = []
    95	            else: seq.append(line.strip())
    96	    if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)
    97	
    98	reps = sorted(os.path.basename(p)[:-3] for p in glob.glob(f"{REPS}/*.fa"))
    99	out = open(f"{OUTD}/scg_raw.faa", "w")
   100	n_rep = n_prot = miss = 0
   101	for rep in reps:
   102	    ap, fp = resolve(rep, "eggnog"), resolve(rep, "bakta")
   103	    if not ap or not fp:
   104	        miss += 1; continue
   105	    hits = scg_proteins(ap)
   106	    if not hits:
   107	        continue
   108	    n_rep += 1
   109	    for pid, s in read_faa(fp):
   110	        g = hits.get(pid)
   111	        if g and len(s) >= 50:
   112	            out.write(f">SCG|{g}|{rep}|{pid}\n{s}\n"); n_prot += 1
   113	out.close()
   114	print(f"[scg] reps={len(reps)} unresolved={miss} reps_with_scg={n_rep} proteins={n_prot}", flush=True)
   115	PY
   116	
   117	echo "[$(date -Iseconds)] cluster SCG at 90% identity"
   118	"$MMSEQS" easy-cluster "$DB/scg_raw.faa" "$DB/scg_c90" "$DB/tmp_scg" --min-seq-id 0.9 -c 0.8 \
   119	    --threads "$T" >/dev/null 2>&1
   120	rm -rf "$DB/tmp_scg"

   122	echo "[$(date -Iseconds)] label marker proteins"
   123	python3 - "$L" "$HYDDB" "$REFS/coxL_formI_refs.faa" "$DB" <<'PY'
   124	import sys, csv, re
   125	L, HYDDB, CORD, OUTD = sys.argv[1:5]
   126	def read_faa(path):
   127	    name, seq = None, []
   128	    with open(path) as fh:
   129	        for line in fh:
   130	            if line.startswith(">"):
   131	                if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)
   132	                name = line[1:].rstrip("\n"); seq = []
   133	            else: seq.append(line.strip())
   134	    if name is not None: yield name, "".join(seq)
   135	safe = lambda s: re.sub(r"[^A-Za-z0-9_.-]", "_", s)
   136	
   137	hyd_class = {}
   138	for r in csv.DictReader(open(f"{L}/classify/atmo_classification.tsv"), delimiter="\t"):
   139	    hyd_class[(r["rep_id"], r["protein_id"], r["marker"])] = r["assignment"]
   140	cox_call = {}
   141	for r in csv.DictReader(open(f"{L}/classify/coxL_class.tsv"), delimiter="\t"):
   142	    cox_call[r["query"]] = r["form_I_call"]
   143	
   144	out = open(f"{OUTD}/markers.faa", "w"); lab = open(f"{OUTD}/marker_labels.tsv", "w")
   145	lab.write("subject\tmarker\tlabel\tsource\n")
   146	n = {}
   147	def emit(sid, seq, marker, label, src):
   148	    out.write(f">{sid}\n{seq}\n"); lab.write(f"{sid}\t{marker}\t{label}\t{src}\n")
   149	    n[(marker, label, src)] = n.get((marker, label, src), 0) + 1
   150	
   151	for name, s in read_faa(f"{L}/queries/hydB.faa"):
   152	    rep, pid = name.split()[0].split("|")
   153	    a = hyd_class.get((rep, pid, "hydB"))
   154	    if a is None:
   155	        continue
   156	    emit(f"EQ|hydB|{rep}|{pid}", s, "hydB", safe(a), "EQ")
   157	for name, s in read_faa(HYDDB):
   158	    # HydDB headers carry the group label; take the [NiFe]/[FeFe]/[Fe] group token
   159	    m = re.search(r"(\[(?:NiFe|FeFe|Fe)\][ _]Group[ _][0-9A-Za-z]+)", name)
   160	    label = safe(m.group(1).replace(" ", "_")) if m else "HydDB_unlabelled"
   161	    emit(f"REF|hydB|{safe(name.split()[0])}", s, "hydB", label, "HydDB")
   162	for name, s in read_faa(f"{L}/queries/coxL.faa"):
   163	    q = name.split()[0]
   164	    c = cox_call.get(q)
   165	    if c is None:
   166	        continue
   167	    rep, pid = q.split("|")
   168	    emit(f"EQ|coxL|{rep}|{pid}", s, "coxL", "form_I" if c == "form_I" else "coxL_other", "EQ")
   169	for name, s in read_faa(CORD):
   170	    emit(f"REF|coxL|{safe(name.split()[0])}", s, "coxL", "form_I", "Cordero2019")
   171	out.close(); lab.close()
   172	for k in sorted(n): print("[label]", *k, n[k], flush=True)
   173	PY
   174

   176	import sys

   179	    for line in fh:

   183	PY
   184	cat "$DB/markers.faa" "$DB/scg_c90_rep_seq.fasta" > "$DB/loa_reads.faa"
   185	"$DIAMOND" makedb --in "$DB/loa_reads.faa" -d "$DB/loa_reads" -p "$T"
   186	python3 - "$DB/loa_reads.faa" > "$DB/subject_lengths.tsv" <<'PY'
   187	import sys
   188	n, L = None, 0
   189	print("subject\tlength_aa")
   190	for line in open(sys.argv[1]):
   191	    if line.startswith(">"):
   192	        if n: print(f"{n}\t{L}")

   194	    else: L += len(line.strip())

   196	PY
