   198	sha256sum "$DB/loa_reads.faa" "$DB/marker_labels.tsv" "$DB/subject_lengths.tsv" > "$DB/db.sha256"
   199	date -Iseconds > "$DB/DB_DONE"
   200	echo "[$(date -Iseconds)] done"
     1	#!/bin/bash
     2	# Life on air (P3), read-based guild abundance -- stage R2: one array task per paired-read sample.
     3	# Subsamples 10 M R1 reads (seqtk, fixed seed), truncates every read to 100 nt so that the
     4	# 101-nt (trip 4) and 150-nt libraries are screened identically, DIAMOND blastx against the
     5	# stage-R1 competitive database, keeps each read's single best hit, and writes per-subject read
     6	# counts at three identity floors (50/60/70%) with alignment length >= 25 aa.
     7	#
     8	#   sbatch --array=1-573%60 r2_reads_array.sh
     9	#
    10	#SBATCH --job-name=loa_reads
    11	#SBATCH --output=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/metagenomics/06_atmospheric/lifeonair_reads/logs/reads_%A_%a.out
    12	#SBATCH --error=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/metagenomics/06_atmospheric/lifeonair_reads/logs/reads_%A_%a.err
    13	#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=16
    14	#SBATCH --mem=48G
    15	#SBATCH --time=06:00:00
    16	#SBATCH --partition=batch
    17	
    18	set -euo pipefail
    19	R=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/metagenomics
    20	A=$R/06_atmospheric/lifeonair_reads
    21	DB=$A/db
    22	MAN=$R/rebuild_20260729/picrust2_current_20260803/coverm_current/prepared/sample_input_manifest.tsv
    23	CONDA=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/miniconda3/envs
    24	DIAMOND=$CONDA/eggnog/bin/diamond
    25	SEQTK=$CONDA/eco_probes/bin/seqtk
    26	T=${SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK:-16}
    27	NSUB=10000000
    28	SEED=11
    29	
    30	[ -s "$DB/DB_DONE" ] || { echo "ERROR: database not built"; exit 1; }
    31	LINE=$(awk -v i="$SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID" 'NR == i + 1' "$MAN")
    32	[ -n "$LINE" ] || { echo "no manifest row for task $SLURM_ARRAY_TASK_ID"; exit 0; }
    33	S=$(cut -f1 <<< "$LINE"); R1=$(cut -f6 <<< "$LINE")
    34	OUT=$A/per_sample_L100/$S
    35	mkdir -p "$OUT"
    36	[ -s "$OUT/DONE" ] && { echo "$S already done"; exit 0; }
    37	
    38	TMP=${SLURM_TMPDIR:-/tmp}/loa_${SLURM_JOB_ID}_${S}
    39	mkdir -p "$TMP"; trap 'rm -rf "$TMP"' EXIT
    40	
    41	echo "[$(date -Iseconds)] $S subsample $NSUB reads (seed $SEED) from $R1"
    42	"$SEQTK" sample -s "$SEED" "$R1" "$NSUB" | "$SEQTK" trimfq -L 100 - | "$SEQTK" seq -A -L 75 - > "$TMP/r1.fa"
    43	NREADS=$(grep -c '^>' "$TMP/r1.fa")
    44	
    45	echo "[$(date -Iseconds)] $S blastx ($NREADS reads)"
    46	"$DIAMOND" blastx -q "$TMP/r1.fa" -d "$DB/loa_reads" -p "$T" --id 50 --evalue 1e-5 \
    47	    --max-target-seqs 1 --block-size 4 --index-chunks 1 --tmpdir "$TMP" \
    48	    --outfmt 6 qseqid sseqid pident length evalue bitscore --quiet -o "$TMP/hits.tsv"
    49	
    50	# one best hit per read (DIAMOND may report several HSPs for the top subject)
    51	awk -F'\t' '$4 >= 25 && !seen[$1]++ {
    52	        c50[$2]++; if ($3 >= 60) c60[$2]++; if ($3 >= 70) c70[$2]++ }
    53	     END { for (s in c50) printf "%s\t%d\t%d\t%d\n", s, c50[s], c60[s] + 0, c70[s] + 0 }' \
    54	    "$TMP/hits.tsv" | sort > "$OUT/subject_counts.tsv"
    55	printf "sample\treads_screened\tR1\tseed\ttruncate_nt\tmin_aln_aa\n%s\t%s\t%s\t%s\t100\t25\n" "$S" "$NREADS" "$R1" "$SEED" > "$OUT/meta.tsv"
    56	date -Iseconds > "$OUT/DONE"
    57	echo "[$(date -Iseconds)] $S done: $(wc -l < "$OUT/subject_counts.tsv") subjects hit"
     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Life on air (P3), read-based guild abundance -- stage R3: per-sample genome-equivalent table.
     3	
     4	For each sample and identity floor (50/60/70%), reads per kilobase (RPK, subject length in nt)
     5	are summed per label; each marker is divided by the median RPK of the 14 single-copy ribosomal
     6	protein genes, giving copies per genome equivalent. For single-copy markers this approximates the
     7	fraction of genomes carrying the capacity.
     8	
     9	    python3 lifeonair/code/r3_aggregate.py lifeonair/results/reads [EXCLUDE_REPS OUT_NAME]
    10	
    11	With EXCLUDE_REPS (one rep id or "rep|protein" id per line), reads whose best hit is an EQ protein of an
    12	excluded rep, or an excluded protein, are discarded before normalisation (contamination and weak-call
    13	sensitivity); output goes to OUT_NAME.
    14	"""
    15	import csv
    16	import statistics
    17	import sys
    18	from collections import Counter
    19	from pathlib import Path
    20	
    21	HA = {"_NiFe__Group_1h": "hydB_1h", "_NiFe__Group_1l": "hydB_1l"}
    22	
    23	
    24	def main(root: Path, exclude: set, out_name: str) -> None:
    25	    lab = {r["subject"]: (r["marker"], r["label"])
    26	           for r in csv.DictReader(open(root / "db/marker_labels.tsv"), delimiter="\t")}
    27	    nt = {r["subject"]: 3 * int(r["length_aa"])
    28	          for r in csv.DictReader(open(root / "db/subject_lengths.tsv"), delimiter="\t")}
    29	    cols = ["sample", "id_floor", "reads_screened", "scg_genes_detected", "scg_median_rpk",
    30	            "hydB_ha", "hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "hydB_other_nife", "coxL_formI", "coxL_other",
    31	            "reads_hydB_1l", "reads_hydB_1h", "reads_hydB_other_nife", "reads_coxL_formI",
    32	            "reads_coxL_other", "reads_scg"]
    33	    out = open(root / out_name, "w")
    34	    out.write("\t".join(cols) + "\n")
    35	    n_done = 0
    36	    for sd in sorted((root / "per_sample").iterdir()):
    37	        if not (sd / "subject_counts.tsv").exists():
    38	            continue
    39	        meta = next(csv.DictReader(open(sd / "meta.tsv"), delimiter="\t"))
    40	        rows = [line.rstrip("\n").split("\t") for line in open(sd / "subject_counts.tsv")]
    41	        n_done += 1
    42	        for k, floor in ((1, 50), (2, 60), (3, 70)):
    43	            rpk, reads = Counter(), Counter()
    44	            for r in rows:
    45	                if exclude and r[0].startswith("EQ|") and (r[0].split("|")[2] in exclude or "|".join(r[0].split("|")[2:4]) in exclude):
    46	                    continue
    47	                m, label = lab[r[0]]
    48	                c = int(r[k])
    49	                if not c:
    50	                    continue
    51	                key = (m, label)
    52	                if m == "hydB":
    53	                    key = ("hydB", HA.get(label, "hydB_other_nife" if "NiFe" in label else "other"))
    54	                elif m == "coxL":
    55	                    key = ("coxL", "coxL_formI" if label == "form_I" else "coxL_other")
    56	                rpk[key] += c / (nt[r[0]] / 1000)
    57	                reads[key] += c
    58	            scg = [v for (m, _), v in rpk.items() if m == "SCG"]
    59	            med = statistics.median(scg) if len(scg) >= 10 else float("nan")
    60	            ge = lambda key: rpk[key] / med if med == med and med > 0 else float("nan")
    61	            ha = ge(("hydB", "hydB_1l")) + ge(("hydB", "hydB_1h"))
    62	            out.write("\t".join(str(x) for x in [
    63	                meta["sample"], floor, meta["reads_screened"], len(scg), f"{med:.2f}",
    64	                f"{ha:.5f}", f"{ge(('hydB', 'hydB_1l')):.5f}", f"{ge(('hydB', 'hydB_1h')):.5f}",
    65	                f"{ge(('hydB', 'hydB_other_nife')):.5f}", f"{ge(('coxL', 'coxL_formI')):.5f}",
    66	                f"{ge(('coxL', 'coxL_other')):.5f}",
    67	                reads[("hydB", "hydB_1l")], reads[("hydB", "hydB_1h")], reads[("hydB", "hydB_other_nife")],
    68	                reads[("coxL", "coxL_formI")], reads[("coxL", "coxL_other")],
    69	                sum(v for (m, _), v in reads.items() if m == "SCG")]) + "\n")
    70	    out.close()
    71	    print(f"aggregated {n_done} samples -> {root / out_name}")
    72	
    73	
    74	if __name__ == "__main__":
    75	    excl = set(open(sys.argv[2]).read().split()) if len(sys.argv) > 2 else set()
    76	    main(Path(sys.argv[1]), excl, sys.argv[3] if len(sys.argv) > 3 else "guild_genome_equivalents.tsv")

exec

  `M/scripts/ibex/82a_atmo_refs.sh`, `82b_atmo_extract.sh`, `82c_atmo_classify.sh`.

## Marker KOs (from 82_metabolism_marker_census.sh — locked, not guessed)
hydB=**K06281** (H2), coxL form-I=**K03520** (CO), coxM K03519, coxS K03518; nif K02588/K02586/K02591; rbcL
K01601; cooS K00198; ectC K06720; otsA K00697; otsB K01087; katE K03781; sodA K04564; phrB K01669; recA K03553.
(Census KO is a first-pass; HydDB phylogeny refines to bona-fide high-affinity.)

## Pipeline (only step 2 is new compute)
1. **Census** — count reps with hydB/coxL KO in rep_ko.tsv. DONE (below).
2. **HydDB typing** (NEW, small) — extract hydB(K06281)/coxL(K03520) proteins of the guild reps from reps.fa /
   the rep proteomes, DIAMOND vs HydDB refs + Cordero form-I refs, mafft+FastTree → keep only high-affinity
   (Group 1l/1h) and form-I. Adapt 82b/82c.
3. **Guild** = reps with high-affinity hydB and/or form-I coxL (per-species, non-redundant).
4. **Abundance** — (a) MAG-direct: join guild reps to profiles_full_recompute abund.tsv over the **551 canonical**
   samples, sum guild abundance / total (decide denominator: whole community incl. unmapped ~64%, vs mapped-only),
   median. (b) 16S: recompute guild prevalence-per-genus on 5,438 × companion-16S abundance (old method = 13.5%).
   **Report BOTH** (they bracket; reviewer faulted 16S-alone).

## Step 1 result — crude-KO census on 5,438 reps (pre-typing)
- hydB K06281 (H2): **1,102 (20.3%)** → `results/hydB_KO_reps.txt`
- coxL form-I K03520 (CO): **1,818 (33.4%)** → `results/coxL_formI_KO_reps.txt`
- both: **519 (9.5%)** ; any (H2 or CO): **2,401 (44.2%)**
- NOTE: crude KO OVER-counts; HydDB typing will drop these to bona-fide high-affinity (old typed numbers on
  15,469 were 3,386 hydB / 3,828 coxL / 1,204 both — expect similar-order after typing, on a per-species basis).

## Decisions / open items
- Abundance method OPEN: 16S (13.5%, old, reviewer-critiqued for only ~64% genus coverage) vs MAG-direct CoverM
  (now available). NO PI doc locks 16S; the plan (RQ.md/D1) actually said "CoverM-weighted". → report both.
- Use dereplicated 5,438 reps (per-species, non-redundant) for census; completeness caveat (MQ miss genes →
  prevalence biased low; HQ-only is the armored version). Capacity, not flux.
- Sample N = **551 canonical** (549 assembly, 542/543 bins). 573 = read-pair superset (CoverM cohort) — subset to
  the 551 for the paper.

# lifeonair_reads: read-based atmospheric trace-gas guild capacity (P3, life on air)

Produced 9-10 October 2026 for the Empty Quarter P3 manuscript (repository
`bio-ontology-research-group/empty-quarter-mags`, code in `code/lifeonair/`, results in `results/lifeonair/`).

| Item | Content |
|---|---|
| `db/` | Competitive DIAMOND database: EQ hydB (K06281) proteins of the 5,438 ANI95 reps labelled by HydDB class, all HydDB references, EQ coxL labelled form I / other (Cordero identity + AYRGAGR), Cordero form-I references, 14 single-copy ribosomal proteins (90% clustered). `db.sha256`. Job 53522301. |
| `per_sample_L100/<sample>/` | Per sample: 10 M R1 reads (seqtk seed 11) truncated to 100 nt, DIAMOND 2.0.15 blastx best hit, alignment >= 25 aa; `subject_counts.tsv` = reads per subject at >= 50/60/70% identity; `meta.tsv`. Array 53524441 (573 samples). |
| `per_sample/` | SUPERSEDED first array 53522544 (alignment >= 40 aa excluded the 101-nt trip-4 libraries). Do not use. |
| `per_sample_counts.tar.gz` | Tarball of `per_sample_L100` as mirrored into the repository (`results/lifeonair/reads/per_sample/`). |
| `operon/` | +/-10-gene Bakta/eggNOG context of every typed hydB/coxL. Job 53524544. |
| `trees/` | mafft --auto + FastTree -lg -gamma trees: hydB (EQ 70% clusters + HydDB NiFe groups 1-2), coxL (EQ >= 600 aa 70% clusters + Cordero refs). Job 53535825 (53524702 failed: selenocysteine U in refs). |
| `coverm_rep_abundance_long.tsv.gz` | Long table of CoverM relative abundance per sample x rep from `rebuild_20260729/picrust2_current_20260803/coverm_current/profiles_full_recompute`. |

Genome equivalents are relative capacity (copies per single-copy-gene genome equivalent), not cell
fractions or activity. Group 1l genomes carry ~1.9 copies on average.

     1	#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
     2	# Life on air (P3): mechanism proxies for H1 (root-adjacent loss of group 1l), 10 Oct 2026.
     3	# Li et al. 2023 (ISME J) attribute the loss of trace-gas oxidisers under desert shrubs to organic carbon
     4	# favouring copiotrophs. Soil organic carbon was never measured in this campaign (P1 has XRF, pH and
     5	# weather only), so two proxies are tested:
     6	#   M1 plant input: NDVI (60-day window, site x trip) against 1l per compartment and against the root effect.
     7	#   M2 copiotrophy: gRodon2 predicted minimum doubling time per representative (g1_growth_rate.sh).
     8	#      a  are 1l carriers slower growers than non-carriers within order?
     9	#      b  is the abundance-weighted community doubling time shorter beside roots (paired site x trip)?
    10	#      c  does the per-pair 1l excess loss scale with that shift?
    11	#      d  genus level: does carrying 1l predict root depletion beyond growth rate?
    12	# Capacity, not activity. Run from the repository root:
    13	#   micromamba run -n eq-lifeonair Rscript lifeonair/code/h1_mechanism.R
    14	
    15	suppressMessages(library(lme4))
    16	IN <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"; OUT <- "lifeonair/results/h1_mechanism"
    17	dir.create(OUT, showWarnings = FALSE, recursive = TRUE)
    18	rd <- function(f, ...) read.delim(f, check.names = FALSE, quote = "", ...)
    19	wt <- function(x, f) write.table(x, file.path(OUT, f), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    20	sp <- function(x, y) { ok <- is.finite(x) & is.finite(y); r <- cor.test(x[ok], y[ok], method = "spearman", exact = FALSE)
    21	  c(rho = unname(r$estimate), p = r$p.value, n = sum(ok)) }
    22	log_lines <- character(); say <- function(...) { s <- sprintf(...); cat(s, "\n"); log_lines <<- c(log_lines, s) }
    23	
    24	meta <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv")); meta <- meta[meta$trip != "" & meta$compartment != "", ]
    25	ge <- rd("lifeonair/results/reads/guild_genome_equivalents.tsv"); ge <- ge[ge$id_floor == 60 & ge$scg_genes_detected >= 10, ]
    26	env <- rd(file.path(IN, "env_site_trip.tsv")); site_env <- rd(file.path(IN, "env_site.tsv"))
    27	d <- merge(meta, ge, by.x = "sample_id", by.y = "sample")
    28	d <- merge(d, env, by = c("site", "trip")); d <- merge(d, site_env[, c("site", "map_era5_mm")], by = "site")
    29	c1 <- rd("lifeonair/results/h123/h1_cell_log2fc_plantroot_vs_deep.tsv")
    30	
    31	# ---- M1 NDVI --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    32	say("M1 NDVI (60-day window) vs group 1l genome equivalents; site NDVI vs MAP rho %.2f",
    33	    sp(aggregate(ndvi_W60d ~ site, d, median)$ndvi_W60d, aggregate(map_era5_mm ~ site, d, median)$map_era5_mm)[1])
    34	m1 <- do.call(rbind, lapply(c("Deep", "PlantRoot", "Surface"), function(cp) {
    35	  x <- d[d$compartment == cp, ]
    36	  m <- lmer(log2(hydB_1l + .01) ~ scale(ndvi_W60d) + scale(log(map_era5_mm)) + (1 | site) + (1 | trip), x)
    37	  cf <- summary(m)$coefficients
    38	  data.frame(compartment = cp, n = nrow(x), ndvi_per_sd = cf[2, 1], ndvi_se = cf[2, 2],
    39	             logmap_per_sd = cf[3, 1], logmap_se = cf[3, 2]) }))
    40	wt(m1, "m1_ndvi_by_compartment.tsv"); print(m1, digits = 3)
    41	cp <- merge(c1, env, by = c("site", "trip"))
    42	mm <- summary(lmer(excess_1l ~ scale(ndvi_W60d) + (1 | site) + (1 | trip), cp))$coefficients
    43	s_cp <- aggregate(cbind(excess_1l, ndvi_W60d) ~ site, cp, median)
    44	m1b <- data.frame(test = c("pair excess_1l ~ NDVI (spearman)", "site-median excess_1l ~ NDVI (spearman)", "pair excess_1l ~ NDVI (lmer, per SD)"),
    45	                  estimate = c(sp(cp$excess_1l, cp$ndvi_W60d)[1], sp(s_cp$excess_1l, s_cp$ndvi_W60d)[1], mm[2, 1]),
    46	                  p_or_se = c(sp(cp$excess_1l, cp$ndvi_W60d)[2], sp(s_cp$excess_1l, s_cp$ndvi_W60d)[2], mm[2, 2]),
    47	                  n = c(nrow(cp), nrow(s_cp), nrow(cp)))
    48	wt(m1b, "m1_root_effect_vs_ndvi.tsv"); print(m1b, digits = 3)
    49	
    50	# ---- M2 growth rate -------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    51	gr_file <- file.path(IN, "rep_growth.tsv")
    52	if (!file.exists(gr_file)) { writeLines(log_lines, file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt")); stop("rep_growth.tsv not yet available") }
    53	gr <- rd(gr_file); gr <- gr[is.finite(gr$d_hours) & gr$d_hours > 0, ]
    54	cls <- rd(file.path(IN, "atmo_classification.tsv")); cx <- rd(file.path(IN, "coxL_class.tsv"))
    55	r1l <- unique(cls$rep_id[cls$marker == "hydB" & grepl("Group_1l$", cls$assignment)])
    56	r1h <- unique(cls$rep_id[cls$marker == "hydB" & grepl("Group_1h$", cls$assignment)])
    57	rfI <- unique(sub("\\|.*", "", cx$query[cx$form_I_call == "form_I"]))
    58	tax <- rbind(rd(file.path(IN, "r232_bac120.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2], rd(file.path(IN, "r232_ar53.summary.tsv"))[, 1:2])
    59	rk <- function(cl, p) ifelse(grepl(paste0(p, "__[^;]"), cl), sub(paste0(".*", p, "__([^;]*).*"), "\\1", cl), "unassigned")
    60	gr$phylum <- rk(tax$classification, "p")[match(gr$rep, tax$user_genome)]
    61	gr$order <- paste(gr$phylum, rk(tax$classification, "o")[match(gr$rep, tax$user_genome)], sep = ";")

    64	gr$logd <- log(gr$d_hours)
    65	say("M2 gRodon estimates for %d representatives (median doubling %.1f h)", nrow(gr), median(gr$d_hours))
    66	
    67	# a carriers vs non-carriers, within order
    68	ma <- lmer(logd ~ c1l + c1h + cfI + (1 | order), gr)
    69	fa <- summary(ma)$coefficients
    70	m2a <- data.frame(term = rownames(fa), fa, row.names = NULL,
    71	                  median_d_carriers = c(NA, median(gr$d_hours[gr$c1l]), median(gr$d_hours[gr$c1h]), median(gr$d_hours[gr$cfI])),
    72	                  median_d_noncarriers = c(NA, median(gr$d_hours[!gr$c1l]), median(gr$d_hours[!gr$c1h]), median(gr$d_hours[!gr$cfI])))
    73	wt(m2a, "m2a_doubling_time_by_carrier_status.tsv"); print(m2a, digits = 3)

    79	ab <- ab[ab$rep != "unmapped" & is.finite(ab$abund) & ab$abund > 0, ]
    80	ab$logd <- gr$logd[match(ab$rep, gr$rep)]
    81	cov_frac <- tapply((!is.na(ab$logd)) * ab$abund, ab$sample, sum) / tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)
    82	ab <- ab[!is.na(ab$logd), ]
    83	cwm <- data.frame(sample_id = names(cov_frac), cwm_logd = as.numeric(tapply(ab$logd * ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)[names(cov_frac)] /

    88	cells <- function(x, a, b, v) { k <- paste(x$site, x$trip); out <- NULL
    89	  for (z in unique(k)) { p <- x[k == z & x$compartment == a, ]; q <- x[k == z & x$compartment == b, ]
    90	    if (nrow(p) && nrow(q)) out <- rbind(out, data.frame(site = p$site[1], trip = p$trip[1], diff = median(p[[v]]) - median(q[[v]]))) }
    91	  out }
    92	db <- cells(d2, "PlantRoot", "Deep", "cwm_logd"); ds <- cells(d2, "Surface", "Deep", "cwm_logd")
    93	sb <- aggregate(diff ~ site, db, median); ss <- aggregate(diff ~ site, ds, median)
    94	m2b <- data.frame(contrast = c("PlantRoot - Deep", "Surface - Deep"), n_pairs = c(nrow(db), nrow(ds)),
    95	                  median_diff_logd = c(median(db$diff), median(ds$diff)),

   139	say("M2c excess_1l vs shift: spearman rho %.2f (p %.2g); lmer slope %.2f (SE %.2f), intercept at no shift %.2f",
   140	    m2c$estimate[1], m2c$p_or_se[1], m2c$estimate[2], m2c$p_or_se[2], m2c$intercept_at_no_shift[2])
   141	f2 <- m2d[m2d$model == "growth + carriage + phylum", ]
   142	say("M2d genus (n=%d): growth slope %.2f (p %.2g); frac_1l %.2f (p %.2g) with phylum",
   143	    nrow(gt), f2$Estimate[f2$term == "median_logd"], f2$Pr...t..[f2$term == "median_logd"],
   144	    f2$Estimate[f2$term == "frac_1l"], f2$Pr...t..[f2$term == "frac_1l"])
   145	writeLines(log_lines, file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt"))
     1	#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
     2	# Life on air (P3): compact environmental inputs for H1-H3, frozen as TSV with checksums.
     3	# Sources (amplicon repository cache; sha256 recorded in lifeonair/results/inputs/env_sources.sha256):
     4	#   climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet  daily ERA5 T and P per site (Open-Meteo, 0.25 deg)
     5	#   metadata_with_rainfall.parquet        per amplicon sample: trip_date (sampling date)
     6	#   ndvi_by_sample.parquet, smap_by_sample.parquet   MODIS NDVI (60 d) and SMAP soil moisture
     7	#   srtm_topo.parquet                     site lat, lon, elevation
     8	# Metagenome sample ids equal the amplicon ids without the "eNNNN_" prefix.
     9	# Outputs: inputs/env_site.tsv (site climate normals), inputs/env_site_trip.tsv (sampling date,
    10	# antecedent precipitation, NDVI/SMAP per site x trip).
    11	#
    12	#   Rscript lifeonair/code/prep_environment.R
    13	
    14	suppressMessages(library(arrow))
    15	C   <- path.expand("~/Documents/papers/empty-quarter-amplicon/repository/cache")
    16	IN  <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"
    17	rd  <- function(f) read.delim(f, sep = "\t", quote = "", stringsAsFactors = FALSE, check.names = FALSE)
    18	
    19	clim <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet")))
    20	clim$date <- as.Date(clim$date)
    21	clim$year <- as.integer(format(clim$date, "%Y"))
    22	ann <- aggregate(P ~ site + year, data = clim, FUN = sum)
    23	site <- data.frame(site = sort(unique(clim$site)))
    24	site$map_era5_mm  <- tapply(ann$P, ann$site, mean)[as.character(site$site)]
    25	site$mat_era5_c   <- tapply(clim$T, clim$site, mean)[as.character(site$site)]
    26	site$rainy_days_per_yr <- tapply(clim$P >= 1, clim$site, sum)[as.character(site$site)] / length(unique(clim$year))
    27	topo <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "srtm_topo.parquet")))
    28	site <- merge(site, topo, by = "site", all.x = TRUE)
    29	
    30	md <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "metadata_with_rainfall.parquet")))
    31	md$sample_id <- sub("^e[0-9]+_", "", md$sample)
    32	md$trip_date <- as.Date(md$trip_date)
    33	nd <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "ndvi_by_sample.parquet")))
    34	sm <- as.data.frame(read_parquet(file.path(C, "smap_by_sample.parquet")))
    35	md <- merge(md, nd, by = "sample", all.x = TRUE); md <- merge(md, sm, by = "sample", all.x = TRUE)
    36	
    37	# antecedent ERA5 precipitation over 90 and 365 days before sampling (NA past the record end)
    38	ante <- function(s, d, days) {
    39	  x <- clim[clim$site == s & clim$date < d & clim$date >= d - days, ]
    40	  if (d - 1 > max(clim$date)) return(NA_real_)
    41	  sum(x$P)
    42	}
    43	# per site x trip: sampling date (earliest amplicon date in that cell) and site-level remote sensing
    44	st <- aggregate(trip_date ~ site + trip, data = md, FUN = min)
    45	st$trip_date <- as.Date(st$trip_date, origin = "1970-01-01")
    46	rs <- aggregate(cbind(ndvi_W60d, evi_W60d, smap_sm_W30d) ~ site + trip, data = md, FUN = median, na.action = na.pass)
    47	st <- merge(st, rs, by = c("site", "trip"), all.x = TRUE)
    48	# antecedent ERA5 precipitation over 90 and 365 days before sampling (NA past the record end)
    49	st$P90_era5  <- mapply(ante, st$site, st$trip_date, 90)
    50	st$P365_era5 <- mapply(ante, st$site, st$trip_date, 365)
    51	st$trip <- paste0("trip", st$trip)
    52	
    53	write.table(site, file.path(IN, "env_site.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    54	write.table(st, file.path(IN, "env_site_trip.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    55	src <- file.path(C, c("climate_historical_1995_2024.parquet", "metadata_with_rainfall.parquet",
    56	                      "ndvi_by_sample.parquet", "smap_by_sample.parquet", "srtm_topo.parquet"))
    57	writeLines(vapply(src, function(f) system2("sha256sum", f, stdout = TRUE), ""), file.path(IN, "env_sources.sha256"))
    58	mg <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv"))
    59	cov <- sum(paste(mg$site, mg$trip) %in% paste(st$site, st$trip))
    60	cat(sprintf("sites %d; site x trip cells %d (P365 available %d); metagenome samples covered %d of %d\n",
    61	            nrow(site), nrow(st), sum(!is.na(st$P365_era5)), cov, nrow(mg)))

/bin/bash -lc 'nl -ba lifeonair/code/decontam_screen.R; nl -ba lifeonair/code/stability_turnover.R; cat lifeonair/code/environment.yml' in /home/leechuck/Documents/papers/empty-quarter-mags

     1	#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
     2	# Life on air (P3), analysis 1: MAG-level contamination screen for the 5,438 ANI95 representatives.
     3	#
     4	# Two independent flags, reported separately and as a union:
     5	#   blank_genus  the representative's GTDB R232 genus (suffix stripped) matches the genus of a
     6	#                Trip-5 16S feature called candidate_contaminant from 17 extraction blanks
     7	#                (data-paper evidence/control-audit/trip5_primary_contaminant_calls.tsv)
     8	#   frequency    decontam frequency method (Davis et al. 2018) re-implemented: among samples with a
     9	#                recorded extraction DNA concentration where the representative is present, compare
    10	#                log(abundance) ~ 1 against log(abundance) ~ offset(-log(conc));
    11	#                score = pf(SS_contam / SS_null, n - 1, n - 1); flagged when score < 0.1, n >= 10.
    12	# Guild abundance (union, hydB, coxL) is then recomputed per sample with and without flagged reps.
    13	#
    14	#   Rscript lifeonair/code/decontam_screen.R   (from the repository root)
    15	
    16	suppressMessages({ library(stats); library(utils) })
    17	IN  <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"
    18	OUT <- "lifeonair/results/decontam"
    19	CTL <- path.expand("~/Public/software/empty-quarter-paper-repos/data-paper/evidence/control-audit/trip5_primary_contaminant_calls.tsv")
    20	dir.create(OUT, showWarnings = FALSE, recursive = TRUE)
    21	
    22	rd <- function(f, ...) read.delim(f, sep = "\t", quote = "", comment.char = "",
    23	                                  stringsAsFactors = FALSE, check.names = FALSE, ...)
    24	
    25	# ---- inputs -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    26	tax <- rbind(rd(file.path(IN, "r232_bac120.summary.tsv"))[, c("user_genome", "classification")],
    27	             rd(file.path(IN, "r232_ar53.summary.tsv"))[, c("user_genome", "classification")])
    28	rank_of <- function(cl, p) { x <- sub(paste0(".*", p, "__([^;]*).*"), "\\1", cl); ifelse(grepl(paste0(p, "__"), cl), x, "") }
    29	tax$genus  <- sub("_[A-Z]+$", "", rank_of(tax$classification, "g"))
    30	tax$phylum <- rank_of(tax$classification, "p")
    31	
    32	ab <- rd(gzfile(file.path(IN, "coverm_rep_abundance_long.tsv.gz")), header = FALSE,
    33	         col.names = c("sample", "rep", "abund"))
    34	ab <- ab[ab$rep != "unmapped" & is.finite(ab$abund) & ab$abund > 0, ]  # F5Dr1 profile is all-NaN
    35	samples <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv"))
    36	reps <- sort(unique(ab$rep))
    37	stopifnot(length(reps) <= 5438)
    38	tax <- tax[tax$user_genome %in% reps, ]
    39	
    40	guild <- list(union = readLines(file.path(IN, "guild_reps.txt")),
    41	              hydB  = readLines(file.path(IN, "guild_hydB_reps.txt")),
    42	              coxL  = readLines(file.path(IN, "guild_coxL_reps.txt")))
    43	
    44	# ---- flag 1: genera of blank-enriched features --------------------------------------------------
    45	ctl <- rd(CTL)
    46	ctl <- ctl[ctl$call == "candidate_contaminant", ]
    47	ctl_genus <- vapply(strsplit(ctl$taxonomy, ";"), function(x) if (length(x) >= 6) x[6] else "", "")
    48	ctl_genus <- sort(unique(ctl_genus[!ctl_genus %in% c("", "NA") & !grepl("^uncultured", ctl_genus)]))
    49	writeLines(ctl_genus, file.path(OUT, "blank_contaminant_genera.txt"))
    50	flag_blank <- tax$user_genome[tax$genus %in% ctl_genus]
    51	
    52	# ---- flag 2: decontam frequency method ----------------------------------------------------------
    53	led <- rd(file.path(IN, "sample_ledger_dna.tsv"))
    54	led$dna_concentration <- suppressWarnings(as.numeric(led$dna_concentration))
    55	led <- led[is.finite(led$dna_concentration) & led$dna_concentration > 0, ]
    56	conc_n <- tapply(led$dna_concentration, led$sample_id, function(x) length(unique(round(x, 6))))
    57	conflict <- names(conc_n)[conc_n > 1]
    58	led <- led[!led$sample_id %in% conflict & !duplicated(led$sample_id), ]
    59	conc <- setNames(led$dna_concentration, led$sample_id)
    60	conc <- conc[names(conc) %in% samples$sample_id]
    61	
    62	abc <- ab[ab$sample %in% names(conc), ]
    63	freq <- do.call(rbind, lapply(split(abc, abc$rep), function(d) {
    64	  n <- nrow(d)
    65	  if (n < 10) return(data.frame(rep = d$rep[1], n_present = n, score = NA_real_))
    66	  y <- log(d$abund); x <- log(conc[d$sample])
    67	  ss0 <- sum((y - mean(y))^2)                 # non-contaminant: abundance independent of input
    68	  r1  <- y + x; ss1 <- sum((r1 - mean(r1))^2) # contaminant: abundance proportional to 1 / input
    69	  data.frame(rep = d$rep[1], n_present = n, score = pf(ss1 / ss0, n - 1, n - 1))
    70	}))
    71	flag_freq <- freq$rep[!is.na(freq$score) & freq$score < 0.1]
    72	
    73	flags <- data.frame(rep = reps)
    74	flags <- merge(flags, tax[, c("user_genome", "phylum", "genus")], by.x = "rep", by.y = "user_genome", all.x = TRUE)
    75	flags <- merge(flags, freq, by = "rep", all.x = TRUE)
    76	flags$flag_blank_genus <- flags$rep %in% flag_blank
    77	flags$flag_frequency   <- flags$rep %in% flag_freq
    78	flags$flag_any         <- flags$flag_blank_genus | flags$flag_frequency
    79	flags$in_guild         <- flags$rep %in% guild$union
    80	write.table(flags, file.path(OUT, "rep_contamination_flags.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    81	
    82	# ---- guild abundance with and without flagged reps ----------------------------------------------
    83	tot <- tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)                        # mapped % of reads (whole = 100)
    84	gab <- function(set, excl = character()) {
    85	  d <- ab[ab$rep %in% setdiff(set, excl), ]
    86	  v <- tapply(d$abund, d$sample, sum); v[setdiff(names(tot), names(v))] <- 0; v[names(tot)]
    87	}
    88	res <- data.frame(sample = names(tot), mapped_pct = as.numeric(tot))
    89	for (g in names(guild)) {
    90	  res[[paste0(g, "_whole")]]          <- as.numeric(gab(guild[[g]]))
    91	  res[[paste0(g, "_whole_clean")]]    <- as.numeric(gab(guild[[g]], flags$rep[flags$flag_any]))
    92	}
    93	mapped_clean <- as.numeric(tot - gab(flags$rep[flags$flag_any]))
    94	for (g in names(guild)) {
    95	  res[[paste0(g, "_mapped")]]       <- 100 * res[[paste0(g, "_whole")]] / res$mapped_pct
    96	  res[[paste0(g, "_mapped_clean")]] <- 100 * res[[paste0(g, "_whole_clean")]] / mapped_clean
    97	}
    98	res <- res[res$mapped_pct > 0, ]
    99	write.table(res, file.path(OUT, "guild_abundance_with_without_flagged.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   100	
   101	# ---- summary -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
   102	q <- function(x) { x <- x[is.finite(x)]; sprintf("%.2f (IQR %.2f-%.2f; n=%d)", median(x), quantile(x, .25), quantile(x, .75), length(x)) }
   103	sink(file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt"))
   104	cat("Life on air: contamination screen of the ANI95 representatives\n")
   105	cat(sprintf("representatives with CoverM abundance: %d\n", length(reps)))
   106	cat(sprintf("blank-enriched candidate_contaminant features: %d; distinct genera: %d\n", nrow(ctl), length(ctl_genus)))
   107	cat(sprintf("samples with a single unambiguous DNA concentration: %d (conflicting values dropped: %d)\n", length(conc), length(conflict)))
   108	cat(sprintf("reps testable by frequency (present in >= 10 such samples): %d\n", sum(!is.na(flags$score))))
   109	cat(sprintf("flagged: blank genus %d, frequency %d, either %d (of which in guild %d of %d)\n",
   110	            sum(flags$flag_blank_genus), sum(flags$flag_frequency), sum(flags$flag_any),
   111	            sum(flags$flag_any & flags$in_guild), length(guild$union)))
   112	cat(sprintf("samples: %d\n", nrow(res)))
   113	for (g in names(guild)) {
   114	  cat(sprintf("%-5s whole-community %%: all %s | clean %s\n", g, q(res[[paste0(g, "_whole")]]), q(res[[paste0(g, "_whole_clean")]])))
   115	  cat(sprintf("%-5s mapped-community %%: all %s | clean %s\n", g, q(res[[paste0(g, "_mapped")]]), q(res[[paste0(g, "_mapped_clean")]])))
   116	}
   117	cat("\nflagged guild members by genus:\n")
   118	fg <- flags[flags$flag_any & flags$in_guild, ]
   119	print(sort(table(paste(fg$phylum, fg$genus)), decreasing = TRUE))
   120	sink()
   121	cat(readLines(file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt")), sep = "\n")
     1	#!/usr/bin/env Rscript
     2	# Life on air (P3), analyses 3 and 4: stability of guild capacity versus turnover of composition.
     3	#
     4	# Measures (per sample):
     5	#   reads   read-based genome equivalents (r3_aggregate.py), identity floor 60%:
     6	#           hydB_ha (group 1h + 1l), coxL_formI
     7	#   mags    CoverM MAG-direct guild abundance, % of mapped community, with and without the
     8	#           contamination-flagged representatives (decontam_screen.R)
     9	# Analysis 3 (stability): lme4 mixed models  y ~ trip + compartment + (1 | site), trip and
    10	#   compartment tested by likelihood ratio (ML fits); marginal variance shares; repeated on the
    11	#   37 sites sampled in >= 4 trips.
    12	# Analysis 4 (turnover): PERMANOVA (vegan::adonis2, Bray-Curtis on Hellinger-free relative
    13	#   abundances, terms trip + site + compartment, by = "margin") for the whole mapped community and
    14	#   for guild carriers only; guild synchrony per site (Loreau & de Mazancourt 2008 phi) across trips
    15	#   at genus level, with a within-site trip-label permutation null.
    16	#
    17	#   Rscript lifeonair/code/stability_turnover.R   (from the repository root)
    18	
    19	suppressMessages({ library(lme4); library(vegan) })
    20	set.seed(20261009)
    21	IN  <- "lifeonair/results/inputs"
    22	OUT <- "lifeonair/results/stability"
    23	dir.create(OUT, showWarnings = FALSE, recursive = TRUE)
    24	rd <- function(f, ...) read.delim(f, sep = "\t", quote = "", comment.char = "",
    25	                                  stringsAsFactors = FALSE, check.names = FALSE, ...)
    26	
    27	meta <- rd(file.path(IN, "samples.tsv"))
    28	meta <- meta[meta$trip != "" & meta$compartment != "", ]
    29	meta$trip <- factor(meta$trip); meta$compartment <- factor(meta$compartment)
    30	meta$site <- factor(meta$site)
    31	trips_per_site <- tapply(meta$trip, meta$site, function(x) length(unique(x)))
    32	long_sites <- names(trips_per_site)[trips_per_site >= 4]
    33	
    34	# ---- measures ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------
    35	measures <- list()
    36	mg <- rd("lifeonair/results/decontam/guild_abundance_with_without_flagged.tsv")
    37	for (v in c("union_mapped", "union_mapped_clean", "hydB_mapped_clean", "coxL_mapped_clean"))
    38	  measures[[paste0("mags_", v)]] <- setNames(mg[[v]], mg$sample)
    39	ge_f <- "lifeonair/results/reads/guild_genome_equivalents.tsv"
    40	if (file.exists(ge_f)) {
    41	  ge <- rd(ge_f); ge <- ge[ge$id_floor == 60, ]
    42	  for (v in c("hydB_ha", "hydB_1l", "hydB_1h", "coxL_formI"))
    43	    measures[[paste0("reads_", v)]] <- setNames(ge[[v]], ge$sample)
    44	}
    45	
    46	# ---- analysis 3: stability ----------------------------------------------------------------------
    47	lrt <- function(full, reduced) { a <- anova(reduced, full); c(chisq = a$Chisq[2], df = a$Df[2], p = a$`Pr(>Chisq)`[2]) }
    48	stab <- list()
    49	for (nm in names(measures)) for (subset in c("all", "sites_ge4_trips")) {
    50	  d <- meta; d$y <- measures[[nm]][d$sample_id]
    51	  d <- d[is.finite(d$y), ]
    52	  if (subset != "all") d <- d[d$site %in% long_sites, ]
    53	  d <- droplevels(d)
    54	  if (nrow(d) < 50) next
    55	  f0 <- lmer(y ~ trip + compartment + (1 | site), data = d, REML = FALSE)
    56	  ft <- lmer(y ~ compartment + (1 | site), data = d, REML = FALSE)
    57	  fc <- lmer(y ~ trip + (1 | site), data = d, REML = FALSE)
    58	  vc <- as.data.frame(VarCorr(f0)); vfix <- var(as.vector(model.matrix(f0) %*% fixef(f0)))
    59	  vtrip <- var(as.vector(model.matrix(f0)[, grep("^trip", colnames(model.matrix(f0))), drop = FALSE] %*%
    60	                         fixef(f0)[grep("^trip", names(fixef(f0)))]))
    61	  tot <- vfix + sum(vc$vcov)
    62	  trip_med <- tapply(d$y, d$trip, median)
    63	  stab[[length(stab) + 1]] <- data.frame(
    64	    measure = nm, subset = subset, n_samples = nrow(d), n_sites = nlevels(d$site),
    65	    median = median(d$y), q25 = quantile(d$y, .25), q75 = quantile(d$y, .75),
    66	    trip_LRT_chisq = lrt(f0, ft)[1], trip_df = lrt(f0, ft)[2], trip_p = lrt(f0, ft)[3],
    67	    compartment_LRT_chisq = lrt(f0, fc)[1], compartment_p = lrt(f0, fc)[3],
    68	    var_share_trip = vtrip / tot, var_share_site = vc$vcov[vc$grp == "site"] / tot,
    69	    var_share_residual = vc$vcov[vc$grp == "Residual"] / tot,
    70	    trip_medians = paste(sprintf("%s=%.3f", names(trip_med), trip_med), collapse = ";"),
    71	    row.names = NULL)
    72	}
    73	stab <- do.call(rbind, stab)
    74	write.table(stab, file.path(OUT, "stability_mixed_models.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    75	
    76	# ---- analysis 4: turnover --------------------------------------------------------------------------
    77	ab <- rd(gzfile(file.path(IN, "coverm_rep_abundance_long.tsv.gz")), header = FALSE,
    78	         col.names = c("sample", "rep", "abund"))
    79	ab <- ab[ab$rep != "unmapped" & is.finite(ab$abund) & ab$abund > 0 & ab$sample %in% meta$sample_id, ]
    80	flags <- rd("lifeonair/results/decontam/rep_contamination_flags.tsv")
    81	ab <- ab[!ab$rep %in% flags$rep[flags$flag_any == "TRUE" | flags$flag_any == TRUE], ]
    82	guild <- readLines(file.path(IN, "guild_reps.txt"))
    83	mat <- xtabs(abund ~ sample + rep, data = ab, sparse = FALSE)
    84	mat <- unclass(mat); mat <- mat[rowSums(mat) > 0, ]
    85	m <- meta[match(rownames(mat), meta$sample_id), ]
    86	
    87	perm <- function(X, label) {
    88	  X <- X[rowSums(X) > 0, , drop = FALSE]; mm <- m[match(rownames(X), m$sample_id), ]
    89	  X <- X / rowSums(X)
    90	  a <- adonis2(vegdist(X, "bray") ~ trip + site + compartment, data = mm, by = "margin", permutations = 999)
    91	  data.frame(community = label, term = rownames(a)[1:3], R2 = a$R2[1:3], F = a$F[1:3], p = a$`Pr(>F)`[1:3],
    92	             n_samples = nrow(X), n_reps = sum(colSums(X) > 0))
    93	}
    94	pm_f <- file.path(OUT, "permanova_turnover.tsv")
    95	if (file.exists(pm_f) && Sys.getenv("LOA_RERUN_PERMANOVA") == "") {
    96	  pm <- rd(pm_f)          # cached: 3 x 999 permutations take ~1.5 h; set LOA_RERUN_PERMANOVA=1 to redo
    97	} else {
    98	  pm <- rbind(perm(mat, "whole_mapped_community"),
    99	              perm(mat[, colnames(mat) %in% guild, drop = FALSE], "guild_carriers"),
   100	              perm(mat[, !colnames(mat) %in% guild, drop = FALSE], "non_guild"))
   101	  write.table(pm, pm_f, sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   102	}
   103	
   104	# synchrony of guild-carrier genera across trips within long-sampled sites.
   105	# Per sample, genus abundance as % of the mapped community; per site x trip, the mean over the
   106	# replicate samples of that trip (all compartments); phi = var(total) / (sum_i sd_i)^2 across trips.
   107	mapped <- tapply(ab$abund, ab$sample, sum)
   108	gl <- ab[ab$rep %in% guild, ]
   109	gen <- flags$genus[match(gl$rep, flags$rep)]; gen[is.na(gen) | gen == ""] <- "unassigned"
   110	gl$genus <- gen
   111	gl$pct <- 100 * gl$abund / as.numeric(mapped[gl$sample])
   112	sg <- xtabs(pct ~ sample + genus, data = gl); sg <- unclass(sg)
   113	sg_meta <- meta[match(rownames(sg), meta$sample_id), ]
   114	phi_of <- function(P) var(rowSums(P)) / sum(apply(P, 2, sd))^2
   115	syn <- do.call(rbind, lapply(long_sites, function(s) {
   116	  idx <- which(sg_meta$site == s)
   117	  if (!length(idx)) return(NULL)
   118	  tr <- droplevels(sg_meta$trip[idx])
   119	  if (nlevels(tr) < 4) return(NULL)
   120	  P <- rowsum(sg[idx, , drop = FALSE], as.character(tr)) / as.numeric(table(as.character(tr)))
   121	  P <- P[, colSums(P) > 0, drop = FALSE]
   122	  tot <- rowSums(P)
   123	  phi <- phi_of(P)
   124	  null <- replicate(999, phi_of(apply(P, 2, sample)))
   125	  cvg <- apply(P, 2, function(v) sd(v) / mean(v))
   126	  data.frame(site = s, n_trips = nrow(P), n_samples = length(idx), n_genera = ncol(P),
   127	             guild_total_mean = mean(tot), cv_guild_total = sd(tot) / mean(tot),
   128	             median_cv_genus = median(cvg), phi = phi, phi_null_median = median(null),
   129	             p_phi_lower = mean(null <= phi))
   130	}))
   131	write.table(syn, file.path(OUT, "guild_synchrony_by_site.tsv"), sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
   132	
   133	sink(file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt"))
   134	cat("== stability (mixed models; LRT for trip and compartment) ==\n")
   135	print(stab[, c("measure", "subset", "n_samples", "n_sites", "median", "trip_p", "compartment_p",
   136	               "var_share_trip", "var_share_site", "var_share_residual")], row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   137	cat("\n== turnover (PERMANOVA, marginal terms) ==\n"); print(pm, row.names = FALSE, digits = 3)
   138	cat(sprintf("\n== guild synchrony across trips, %d sites with >= 4 trips ==\n", nrow(syn)))
   139	cat(sprintf("median phi %.3f (null median %.3f); sites with phi below null at p<0.05: %d\n",
   140	            median(syn$phi), median(syn$phi_null_median), sum(syn$p_phi_lower < 0.05)))
   141	cat(sprintf("median CV guild total %.3f vs median mean-CV of carrier genera %.3f\n",
   142	            median(syn$cv_guild_total), median(syn$median_cv_genus)))
   143	sink()
   144	cat(readLines(file.path(OUT, "SUMMARY.txt")), sep = "\n")

     1	#!/bin/bash
     2	# Life on air (P3), mechanism proxy for H1: predicted maximum growth rate (copiotrophy) per representative.
     3	#   Li et al. 2023 (ISME J) tie the loss of trace-gas oxidisers under shrubs to organic carbon favouring
     4	#   copiotrophs. Soil organic carbon was never measured here, so we use the same life-history proxy:
     5	#   gRodon2 codon-usage-bias minimum doubling time (Weissman et al. 2021), mode "partial" (MAGs),
     6	#   highly expressed set = Bakta CDS annotated as ribosomal proteins under either naming scheme
     7	#   ("50S ribosomal protein L2" or "Large ribosomal subunit protein uL2"); the first run (53541016) matched only the
     8	#   old names and left 2,824 reps without an estimate.
     9	#   Input: the 5,438 ANI95 representatives, Bakta .ffn/.faa resolved as in c1_operon_context.sh.
    10	#   Output: growth/rep_growth.tsv (rep, n_cds, n_ribo, d_hours, lower, upper, cub_hegs).
    11	#
    12	#   sbatch g1_growth_rate.sh
    13	#
    14	#SBATCH --job-name=loa_growth
    15	#SBATCH --output=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/metagenomics/06_atmospheric/lifeonair_reads/logs/growth_%j.out
    16	#SBATCH --error=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/metagenomics/06_atmospheric/lifeonair_reads/logs/growth_%j.err
    17	#SBATCH --cpus-per-task=16
    18	#SBATCH --mem=32G
    19	#SBATCH --time=12:00:00
    20	#SBATCH --partition=batch
    21	
    22	set -euo pipefail
    23	R=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/metagenomics
    24	OUT=$R/06_atmospheric/lifeonair_reads/growth
    25	ENV=$R/06_atmospheric/lifeonair_reads/envs/grodon
    26	mkdir -p "$OUT/ffn" "$(dirname "$ENV")"
    27	CONDA=/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/rob/miniconda3
    28	source "$CONDA/bin/activate" base
    29	T=${SLURM_CPUS_PER_TASK:-16}
    30	
    31	if [[ ! -x "$ENV/bin/Rscript" ]]; then
    32	  conda create -y -p "$ENV" -c conda-forge -c bioconda r-base=4.3 bioconductor-biostrings \
    33	      bioconductor-cordon r-matrixstats r-remotes r-data.table >/dev/null
    34	  "$ENV/bin/Rscript" -e 'remotes::install_github("jlw-ecoevo/gRodon2", upgrade = "never", quiet = TRUE)'
    35	fi
    36	"$ENV/bin/Rscript" -e 'cat("gRodon", as.character(packageVersion("gRodon")), packageDescription("gRodon")$RemoteSha, "\n")' | tee "$OUT/grodon_version.txt"
    37	
    38	# 1. per rep: CDS-only ffn (ids present in .faa), ribosomal-protein flag in the header
    39	conda activate bakta
    40	python3 - "$R" "$OUT" <<'PY'
    41	import csv, glob, os, re, sys
    42	R, OUT = sys.argv[1:3]
    43	TREE = "/ibex/scratch/projects/c2014/EmptyQuarter_Data/processed/sequencing-results/sites"
    44	REPS = f"{R}/05_refine/_drep/out/dereplicated_genomes"
    45	meta = {r["sample"]: r for r in csv.DictReader(open(f"{R}/metadata/sample_metadata.tsv"), delimiter="\t")}
    46	def resolve(rep):                             # identical resolver to c1_operon_context.sh
    47	    sample = rep.split("__")[0]; m = meta.get(sample)
    48	    if "__" not in rep or not m: return None
    49	    tok, local = rep.split("__", 1)[1].split("_", 1)
    50	    anno = "single_sample" if tok == "single" else "self_supervised"
    51	    base = f"{TREE}/{m['trip']}/site{m['site']}/{sample}/wgs/annotation/{anno}"
    52	    for lc in [local] + ([local[:-4]] if local.endswith("_sub") else []):
    53	        p = f"{base}/bakta/{lc}/{lc}"
    54	        if os.path.exists(p + ".ffn") and os.path.exists(p + ".faa"): return p
    55	    return None
    56	RIBO = re.compile(r"^((30S|50S) ribosomal protein|(Large|Small) ribosomal subunit protein) ")   # old and new UniProt names
    57	reps = sorted(os.path.basename(p)[:-3] for p in glob.glob(f"{REPS}/*.fa"))
    58	miss = 0
    59	for rep in reps:
    60	    p = resolve(rep)
    61	    if not p: miss += 1; continue
    62	    cds = {l[1:].split()[0] for l in open(p + ".faa") if l.startswith(">")}
    63	    with open(f"{OUT}/ffn/{rep}.ffn", "w") as o:
    64	        keep = False
    65	        for line in open(p + ".ffn"):
    66	            if line.startswith(">"):
    67	                tag, _, prod = line[1:].strip().partition(" ")
    68	                keep = tag in cds
    69	                if keep: o.write(f">{tag} {'HEG' if RIBO.match(prod) else 'x'}\n")
    70	            elif keep: o.write(line)
    71	print(f"[ffn] reps={len(reps)} unresolved={miss}", flush=True)
    72	PY
    73	
    74	# 2. gRodon per rep, parallel
    75	cat > "$OUT/run_grodon.R" <<'RS'
    76	suppressMessages({library(gRodon); library(Biostrings); library(parallel)})
    77	args <- commandArgs(TRUE); files <- list.files(args[1], "\\.ffn$", full.names = TRUE)
    78	one <- function(f) {
    79	  s <- readDNAStringSet(f); s <- s[width(s) %% 3 == 0 & width(s) >= 240]
    80	  heg <- grepl(" HEG$", names(s))
    81	  rep <- sub("\\.ffn$", "", basename(f))
    82	  if (sum(heg) < 10) return(data.frame(rep, n_cds = length(s), n_ribo = sum(heg), d_hours = NA, lower = NA, upper = NA, cub_hegs = NA))
    83	  g <- tryCatch(predictGrowth(s, heg, mode = "partial"), error = function(e) NULL)
    84	  if (is.null(g)) return(data.frame(rep, n_cds = length(s), n_ribo = sum(heg), d_hours = NA, lower = NA, upper = NA, cub_hegs = NA))
    85	  data.frame(rep, n_cds = length(s), n_ribo = sum(heg), d_hours = g$d, lower = g$LowerCI, upper = g$UpperCI, cub_hegs = g$CUBHE)
    86	}
    87	res <- do.call(rbind, mclapply(files, one, mc.cores = as.integer(args[3])))
    88	write.table(res, args[2], sep = "\t", quote = FALSE, row.names = FALSE)
    89	cat("[grodon] reps", nrow(res), "with estimate", sum(!is.na(res$d_hours)), "\n")
    90	RS
    91	"$ENV/bin/Rscript" "$OUT/run_grodon.R" "$OUT/ffn" "$OUT/rep_growth.tsv" "$T"
    92	rm -rf "$OUT/ffn"
    93	sha256sum "$OUT/rep_growth.tsv" > "$OUT/rep_growth.sha256"
    94	date -Iseconds > "$OUT/GROWTH_DONE"
     1	#!/usr/bin/env python3
     2	"""Life on air (P3), analysis 5 summary: operon features of typed hydB/coxL by class.
     3	
     4	Reports all loci and, separately, loci with >= 5 genes on both sides on the contig (complete
     5	context), because absent partners near a contig end mostly reflect assembly fragmentation.
     6	Also lists the most frequent neighbours within +/-5 genes of high-affinity hydB.
     7	
     8	    python3 lifeonair/code/operon_summary.py lifeonair/results/operon
     9	"""
    10	import csv
    11	import gzip
    12	import sys
    13	from collections import Counter, defaultdict
    14	from pathlib import Path
    15	
    16	
    17	def klass(r):
    18	    c = r["class"]
    19	    if r["marker"] == "hydB":
    20	        return "hydB_1h" if c.endswith("Group_1h") else "hydB_1l" if c.endswith("Group_1l") else "hydB_other"
    21	    return "coxL_formI" if c == "form_I" else "coxL_other"
    22	
    23	
    24	def pct(v, f):
    25	    return f"{100 * sum(map(f, v)) / len(v):.1f}" if v else "nan"
    26	
    27	
    28	def main(d: Path) -> None:
    29	    rows = list(csv.DictReader(open(d / "marker_context.tsv"), delimiter="\t"))
    30	    groups = defaultdict(list)
    31	    for r in rows:
    32	        k = klass(r)
    33	        groups[(k, "all")].append(r)
    34	        if int(r["genes_to_contig_end"]) >= 5:
    35	            groups[(k, "complete_context")].append(r)
    36	    with open(d / "operon_summary.tsv", "w") as o:
    37	        o.write("class\tsubset\tn\tsmall_subunit_adjacent_pct\tany_maturation_pct\tcoxM_pct\tcoxS_pct\t"
    38	                "coxMSL_pct\tcox_accessory_pct\tany_hypothetical_pct\n")
    39	        for (k, s), v in sorted(groups.items()):
    40	            o.write("\t".join([k, s, str(len(v)),
    41	                               pct(v, lambda r: r["small_subunit_adjacent"] == "1"),
    42	                               pct(v, lambda r: int(r["n_maturation"]) > 0),
    43	                               pct(v, lambda r: r["coxM"] == "1"), pct(v, lambda r: r["coxS"] == "1"),
    44	                               pct(v, lambda r: r["coxM"] == "1" and r["coxS"] == "1"),
    45	                               pct(v, lambda r: int(r["n_cox_accessory"]) > 0),
    46	                               pct(v, lambda r: int(r["n_hypothetical"]) > 0)]) + "\n")
    47	    ctx = {(r["rep"], r["protein_id"]): klass(r) for r in rows}
    48	    nb = Counter(); nloc = Counter()
    49	    for r in csv.DictReader(gzip.open(d / "neighbours.tsv.gz", "rt"), delimiter="\t"):
    50	        k = ctx[(r["rep"], r["protein_id"])]
    51	        off = int(r["offset"])
    52	        if off == 0:
    53	            nloc[k] += 1
    54	            continue
    55	        if abs(off) > 5 or k not in ("hydB_1h", "hydB_1l"):
    56	            continue
    57	        name = r["eggnog_name"] if r["eggnog_name"] not in ("-", "") else (r["bakta_gene"] or r["bakta_product"])
    58	        nb[(k, name)] += 1
    59	    with open(d / "hydB_ha_top_neighbours.tsv", "w") as o:
    60	        o.write("class\tneighbour\tcount\tper_locus\n")
    61	        for (k, name), c in nb.most_common():
    62	            if c >= 20:
    63	                o.write(f"{k}\t{name}\t{c}\t{c / nloc[k]:.3f}\n")
    64	    print(open(d / "operon_summary.tsv").read())
    65	
    66	
    67	if __name__ == "__main__":
    68	    main(Path(sys.argv[1]))

This article is published with open access Abstract Diverse aerobic bacteria persist by consuming atmospheric hydrogen (H2) using group 1h [NiFe]-hydrogenases. However, other hydrogenase classes are also distributed in aerobes, including the group 2a [NiFe]-hydrogenase. Based on studies focused on Cyanobacteria, the reported physiological role of the group 
p 1 bacteria persist by consuming atmospheric hydrogen (H2) using group 1h [NiFe]-hydrogenases. However, other hydrogenase classes are also distributed in aerobes, including the group 2a [NiFe]-hydrogenase. Based on studies focused on Cyanobacteria, the reported physiological role of the group 2a [NiFe]-hydrogenase is to recycle H2 produced by nitrogenase. However, given this hydrogenase is also present in various heterotrophs and l
p 1 ogenase classes are also distributed in aerobes, including the group 2a [NiFe]-hydrogenase. Based on studies focused on Cyanobacteria, the reported physiological role of the group 2a [NiFe]-hydrogenase is to recycle H2 produced by nitrogenase. However, given this hydrogenase is also present in various heterotrophs and lithoautotrophs lacking nitrogenases, it may play a wider role in bacterial metabolism. Here we investigated the
p 1 s ferrooxidans (phylum Proteobacteria), Chloroﬂexus aggregans (phylum Chloroﬂexota), and Gemmatimonas aurantiaca (phylum Gemmatimonadota). qRT-PCR analysis revealed that the group 2a [NiFe]-hydrogenase of all three species is signiﬁcantly upregulated during exponential growth compared to stationary phase, in contrast to the proﬁle of the persistence-linked group 1h [NiFe]-hydrogenase. p 8 s study also identiﬁes key microbial and enzymatic players in the global hydrogen cycle. p 8 cross multiple bacterial phyla. The group 1h enzyme is probably the main sink of the H2 cycle given it is the predominant hydrogenase in most soils [4, 11, 61]. codex
I found a potentially decisive classification error: the CoxL pipeline labels `AYRGAGR` as form I, but the literature identifies it as a form-II motif. That affects the CO comparison and one of the H1 controls. The temporal cohort also excludes expedition 5: it covers March 2023–August 2024, about 17 months. I’m quantifying both issues and checking which results remain defensible.

dp = os.path.join(OUT, "coxL_diamond.tsv")
if os.path.exists(dp):
    with open(dp) as fh:

FORM_I  = re.compile(r"AYRGAGR")
FORM_II = re.compile(r"AYR[CG]SFR")
def motif_call(seq):
    if FORM_I.search(seq):  return "AYRGAGR(formI)"
    if FORM_II.search(seq): return "AYRxSFR(formII)"
    return "none"
with open(os.path.join(OUT, "coxL_class.tsv"), "w") as o:
    o.write("query\tlength\tpident_formI\tqcov_formI\tmotif\tform_I_call\n")

        h = cox.get(name, {}); pid = h.get("pident", 0.0); qc = h.get("qcov", 0.0)
        mot = motif_call(seq); thr = (pid > 55.0 and qc >= 90.0)
        call = "form_I" if (thr or mot == "AYRGAGR(formI)") else ("form_II/other" if mot == "AYRxSFR(formII)" else "ambiguous")
        if call == "form_I": n_formI += 1
        if mot == "AYRGAGR(formI)": n_motifI += 1
        o.write(f"{name}\t{len(seq)}\t{pid:.1f}\t{qc:.1f}\t{mot}\t{call}\n")

FILE fertile-islands_2023_trace-gas-oligotrophs.pdf pages 10
ARTICLE Reduced trace gas oxidizers as a response to organic carbon availability linked to oligotrophs in desert fertile islands Shuyue Li1, Shanshan Yang1, Xiaomeng Wei2, Shuo Jiao 1, Wen Luo1, Weimin Chen 1✉and Gehong Wei 1✉ © The Author(s), under exclusive licence to International Society for Microbial Ecology 2023 Atmospheric trace gases, such as H2 and CO, are important energy sources for microbial growth and maintenance in various ecosystems, especially in arid deserts with little organic substrate. Nonetheless, the impact of soil organic C availability on microbial trace gas oxidation and the underlying mechanisms are unclear at the community level. This study investigated the energy and life- history strategies of soil microbiomes along an organic C gradient inside and out of Hedysarum scoparium islands dispersed in the Mu Us Desert, China. Metagenomic analysis showed that with increasing organic C availability from bare areas into “fertile islands”, the abundance of trace gas oxidizers (TGOs) decreased, but that of trace gas nonoxidizers (TGNOs) increased. The variation in their abundance was more related to labile/soluble organic C levels than to stable/insoluble organic C levels. The consumption rates of H2 and CO conﬁrmed that organic C addition, especially soluble organic C addition, inhibited microbial trace gas oxidation. Moreover, microorganisms with distinct energy-acquiring strategies showed different life-history traits. The TGOs had lower 16 S rRNA operon copy numbers, lower predicted maximum growth rates and higher proportions of labile C degradation genes, implying the prevalence of oligotrophs. In contrast, copiotrophs were prevalent in the TGNOs. These results revealed a mechanism for the microbial community to adapt to the highly heterogeneous distribution of C resources by adjusting the abundances of taxa with distinct energy and life-history strategies, which would further affect trace gas consumption and C turnover in desert ecosystems. The ISME Journal (2023) 17:1257–1266; https://doi.org/10.1038/s41396-023-01437-6 INTRODUCTION In desert ecosystems, organic C limitation, aridity, and other physicochemical pressures restrict the residence of living organ- isms [1, 2]; however, the capacity to acquire energy from atmospheric trace gases such as H2 and CO beneﬁts the survival of some microorganisms in these extremely barren ecosystems [3, 4]. Previous pure culture studies have proven that when organic C is depleted, organoheterotrophic isolates upregulate hydrogenase and CO dehydrogenase expression to obtain alternative energy for cellular maintenance [5–7]. These ﬁndings emphasize the relationship between microbial trace gas oxidation and environmental organic C levels. A community-based study across a desert aridity gradient showed that the hydrogenase gene abundance and the ex situ H2 oxidation rate signiﬁcantly decreased with increasing soil organic C content [8]. Another study including various ecosystems showed that forests, wetlands and grasslands, which tend to have relatively high organic C reserves, had higher gene abundances and oxidation rates for trace gases than drylands [9]. These inconsistent results require more studies to explore the inﬂuence of organic C availability on microbial trace gas oxidation at the community level, which can help reveal the change in microbial energy sources between organic compounds and inorganic trace gases under changing nutritional conditions in natural environments. Organic C availability is dependent on recalcitrance to microbial decomposition; thus, organic C is categorized as stable or labile [10]. Dissolved organic C is probably the most degradable fraction of labile organic C because the soluble state is a prerequisite for microbes to absorb substrates through cell membranes, and solid matter can only be degraded after it is dissolved or hydrolyzed by exoenzymes [11]. Fanin et al. [12] showed a greater inﬂuence of soluble organic matter on microorganisms, as indicated by the stronger correlation between microbial C:P and N:P ratios and elemental stoichiometries of soluble litter fractions than insoluble fractions. As the soluble fraction is more readily bioavailable than the insoluble fraction, solubility and stability should be considered when exploring the relationship between soil organic C availability and trace gas oxidation. The availability of environmental organic C is usually considered to inﬂuence the life-history strategies of microorganisms. Copio- trophs are associated with resource-rich environments, and they can grow rapi

FILE lopez_2023_growth-rate-dominant-factor.pdf pages 10
ARTICLE OPEN Growth rate is a dominant factor predicting the rhizosphere effect José L. López 1,2,3, Arista Fourie 1, Sanne W. M. Poppeliers4, Nikolaos Pappas 1, Juan J. Sánchez-Gil 4, Ronnie de Jonge 4 and Bas E. Dutilh 1,3✉ © The Author(s) 2023 The root microbiome is shaped by plant root activity, which selects speciﬁc microbial taxa from the surrounding soil. This inﬂuence on the microorganisms and soil chemistry in the immediate vicinity of the roots has been referred to as the rhizosphere effect. Understanding the traits that make bacteria successful in the rhizosphere is critical for developing sustainable agriculture solutions. In this study, we compared the growth rate potential, a complex trait that can be predicted from bacterial genome sequences, to functional traits encoded by proteins. We analyzed 84 paired rhizosphere- and soil-derived 16S rRNA gene amplicon datasets from 18 different plants and soil types, performed differential abundance analysis, and estimated growth rates for each bacterial genus. We found that bacteria with higher growth rate potential consistently dominated the rhizosphere, and this trend was conﬁrmed in different bacterial phyla using genome sequences of 3270 bacterial isolates and 6707 metagenome-assembled genomes (MAGs) from 1121 plant- and soil-associated metagenomes. We then identiﬁed which functional traits were enriched in MAGs according to their niche or growth rate status. We found that predicted growth rate potential was the main feature for differentiating rhizosphere and soil bacteria in machine learning models, and we then analyzed the features that were important for achieving faster growth rates, which makes bacteria more competitive in the rhizosphere. As growth rate potential can be predicted from genomic data, this work has implications for understanding bacterial community assembly in the rhizosphere, where many uncultivated bacteria reside. The ISME Journal (2023) 17:1396–1405; https://doi.org/10.1038/s41396-023-01453-6 INTRODUCTION Soils represent the most complex and diverse microbiomes in the world. A notable component of these is the rhizosphere, comprising the soil region near plant roots, which are inﬂuenced by root exudates and rhizodeposition [1]. Rhizosphere microbiomes assem- ble by enriching a subset of the microbiota present in the surrounding bulk soil, which may pose beneﬁcial, neutral, or detrimental effects on the host plants [1, 2]. The changes in microbial community toward the rhizosphere, known as the “rhizosphere effect” [3], involves a decrease in species richness imposed by stronger selection [4]. Rhizosphere communities display signiﬁcant variation across experimental settings, exhibiting distinctive or overlapping microbial composition compared to the bulk soil [4–10]. While methodological differences between these studies cannot be ruled out, including biases during rhizosphere microbiome isolation, there may also be physiological factors that inﬂuence the rhizosphere effect [3]. On the plant side, the rhizosphere effect may be inﬂuenced by the host plant species [11], the stage of the plant life cycle [12], or the location on the root [8, 13]. Understanding the biological factors driving rhizosphere com- munity assembly has been a complex and intricate endeavor [3]. Approaches to studying rhizosphere-associated traits have included analyzing changes in bacterial composition or function between roots and soils [14–19]. A comparative analysis of thousands of genomes from isolates obtained in plant-associated and non-plant-associated environments has identiﬁed genomic traits associated with plant colonization [14], but analysis of unculturable bacteria was previously lacking. Besides taxonomic or functional changes in the rhizosphere microbiome, growth rate potential has been suggested as an ecological indicator of microbial lifestyle in the soil [20]. Copiotrophs, adapted to high nutrient conditions with faster growth rates, are enriched in soils with abundant labile organic substrates (i.e., glycine, sucrose), while oligotrophs, adapted to low nutrient conditions with slower but more efﬁcient growth, are enriched in soils containing recalcitrant chemicals (i.e., cellulose, lignin, or tannin–protein) [21]. Agricultural inputs of nitrogen and phosphorus have also been associated with increased relative abundance of copiotrophic bacteria [22]. These observations suggest that plant root exudates may contribute to the selection of copiotrophs in the rhizosphere, playing a role in establishing the rhizosphere effect. Recently, a model based

Bainard3 and Etienne Yergeau4* 1 Evolutionary Ecology of Plants, Faculty of Biology, Philipps-University Marburg, Marburg, Germany, 2 Energy, Mining and Environment, National Research Council Canada, Montréal, QC, Canada, 3 Swift Current Research and Development Centre, Agriculture and Agri-Food Canada, Swift Current, SK, Canada, 4 Centre Armand-Frappier Santé Biotechnologie, Institut National de la Recherche Scientiﬁque, Laval, QC, Canada Next-generation sequencing is one of the most popular and cost-effective ways of characterizing microbiome in multiple samples. Comparing relative and estimated absolute abundances of dominant bacterial and fungal phyla, as well as their correlation with the functional processes in the rhizosphere, our results suggest that the estimated absolute abundance approach gives a different and more realistic perspective than the relative abundance approach. Such a quantiﬁcation approach provides complementary information that helps to better understand the rhizosphere microbiomes and their associated ecological functional processes. Article https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-024-46920-6 Survival and rapid resuscitation permit limited productivity in desert microbial communities Stefanie Imminger 1,2,7, Dimitri V. Meier1,6,7, Arno Schintlmeister 1,3, Anton Legin 4, Jörg Schnecker 1, Andreas Richter 1, Osnat Gillor 5, Stephanie A. Eichorst 1 & Dagmar Woebken 1 Microbial activity in drylands tends to be conﬁned to rare and short periods of rain. Rapid growth should be key to the maintenance of ecosystem processes in such narrow activity windows, if desiccation and rehydration cause wide- spread cell death due to osmotic stress. Here, simulating rain with 2H2O fol- lowed by single-cell NanoSIMS, we show that biocrust microbial communities in the Negev Desert are characterized by limited productivity, with median replication times of 6 to 19 days and restricted number of days allowing growth. Genome-resolved metatranscriptomics reveals that nearly all micro- bial populations resuscitate within minutes after simulated rain, independent of taxonomy, and invest their activity into repair and energy generation. Together, our data reveal a community that makes optimal use of short activity phases by fast and universal resuscitation enabling the maintenance of key ecosystem functions. We conclude that desert biocrust communities are highly adapted to surviving rapid changes in soil moisture and solute con- centrations, resulting in high persistence that balances limited productivity. Drylands constitute 46% of the terrestrial surface1,2, play an important role in the global carbon cycle3 and are currently expanding due to climatic changes4,5. They are characterized by long periods of drought, with rare and short rain events, that last only a few days a year6–9. As the activity of desert microorganisms is largely conﬁned to these short and unpredictable episodes of rain10, so are main microbial-mediated ecosystem processes in desert soils11. However, it remains unclear how microbialfunctions are executed if a considerable proportionof desert soil microorganisms remain dormant after rehydration, or if they exhibit considerable delay in their response as reported in other semiarid soils12,13. Key information, such as the fraction and identity of responding desert soil microorganisms and their resuscitation speed, is lacking. Further, information on desert soil microbial productivity is lacking, despite its importance in being linked to the dryland’s con- tribution to the global carbon cycle3. It has been suggested that dry- down and/or rapid rehydration of soils via rain can cause cell death14, which in turn would necessitate rapid growth in the short window of rain-induced activity for maintaining desert soil microbial commu- nities. However, in situ growth rate data from desert soil micro- organisms that support this assumption are lacking. Article https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-026-76323-8 Widespread horizontal transfer and strong selection enhance microbial adaptation in Antarctic soils Yongyi Peng 1,2 , Laura C. Woods1,2, Laura Perlaza-Jimenez2,3, Rachael Lappan 1,2, Marion Jespersen2, Xiyang Dong 4, S. Ry Holland 1,2, Steven L. Chown 1,5, Pok Man Leung 1,2 & Chris Greening 1,2,3 Terrestrial Antarctica harbors compositionally diverse and functionally dis- tinct microbial life. Yet the eco-evolutionary processes underlying adaptation to Antarctica’s polyextreme conditions remain largely unknown. Here, we address how horizontal gene transfer (HGT) and de novo mutations inﬂuence microbial adaptation in 16 Antarctic soils using combined short- and long-read datasets. Phylogenetic reconciliation and mobile genetic element analysis of 676 metagenome-assembled genomes show frequent HGT across commu- nities. While transferred genes span diverse functional categories, those involved in energy metabolism are exchanged at higher frequency. Genes for aerotrophy, i.e. the consumption of atmospheric trace gases to provide energy, carbon, and hydration, are among the most frequently disseminated. Approximately a quarter of carbon monoxide dehydrogenases and [NiFe]- hydrogenases are predicted to be horizontally acquired and are often asso- ciated with mobile genetic elements. Analysis of polymorphisms suggests widespread purifying selection, particularly for aerotrophy genes, providing further evidence that aerotrophy is critical for microbial survival in Antarctica. Genetic variation in hydrogenases is tightly associated with predicted protein structures, with intense selection acting on critical sites preserving stability and function. Together, these ﬁndings show that previously unrecognized eco- evolutionary dynamics shape the composition and function of Antarctic microbial communities, and conﬁrm aerotrophy is a strongly selected and horizontally disseminated trait. Once regarded as a frozen desert with limited signs of life, Antarctica is increasingly recognized as a continent populated with metabolically active and taxonomically diverse microbial communities1–3. What remains unclear, however, is how this diversity arises and is maintained under the strong constraints imposed by the Antarctic environment. One potential mechanism is genetic diversiﬁcation through de novo mutations and the acquisition of foreign DNA through horizontal gene transfer (HGT), which governs both ancient and contemporary bac- terial evolution4–6. FILE reji_2026_moisture-threshold-H2-uptake.pdf pages 12
Article https://doi.org/10.1038/s41467-025-67208-3 Global implications of a low soil moisture threshold for microbial hydrogen uptake Linta Reji 1,2,5 , Matteo B. Bertagni 1,3,6, Fabien Paulot 4, Qianhui Qin 1,2 & Xinning Zhang 1,2 The impact of increasing anthropogenic hydrogen (H2) emissions on Earth’s radiative balance depends on the soil microbial H2 sink—the largest and most uncertain term in the global H2 budget. Soil moisture is a primary but poorly quantiﬁed control regulating the soil sink. Here, we assess the sensitivity of microbial H2 oxidation to soil moisture in laboratory experiments with tem- perate and arid soils spanning distinct textures. We report H2 oxidizer activity down to –70 to –100 MPa water potentials across soils, which are among the driest conditions reported for microbial activity and are much drier than assumed in global simulations of H2. Using genome-resolved meta-omics, we link H2 oxidation dynamics in temperate soils to speciﬁc desiccation-adapted microbial taxa that contribute differentially to H2 uptake along the moisture gradient. Through global simulations, we show that our observationally con- strained drier moisture threshold increases the contribution of arid and semi- arid regions for soil H2 uptake by 4-7 percentage points (pp), while decreasing the contribution of temperate and continental regions (−7 pp). Our results highlight the importance of H2 uptake under extreme hydrological conditions, particularly the roles of desertiﬁcation, dryland expansion, and H2-oxidizer ecophysiology in modulating long-term changes in H2 uptake. Molecular hydrogen (H2) is an abundant trace gas in the troposphere, with a current global mean concentration of ~550 ppb in the marine boundary layer1. H2 is expected to play a key role in the global clean energy transition due to its potential as a low-carbon fuel2,3. However, an increasing reliance on H2 energy will likely raise atmospheric H2 concentrations, as H2 is leak-prone4,5. This warrants careful evaluation of the potential climate effects of increasing H2 levels in the atmo- sphere. While H2 is not a greenhouse gas, it still imparts a signiﬁcant indirect radiative forcing due to its interaction with hydroxyl (OH) radicals in the atmosphere. This interaction prolongs the lifetime of tropospheric methane and contributes to the production of strato- spheric water vapor and tropospheric ozone6–11. Given the signiﬁcance of H2 for Earth’s radiative balance (100-year time-horizon Global Warming Potential, GWP100, estimated to be 11.6 ± 2.811), the balance of the global H2 budget is a critical consideration in the context of accelerated energy transitions. Uptake by soil microbes is the largest sink for atmospheric H2, accounting for 60–80% of the global tropospheric H2 sink8,12–14. By reducing the amount of H2 available to react with OH radicals in the atmosphere, the soil sink considerably dampens the indirect radiative impact of H2. Recent culture-based and genomic work has established that soil uptake is microbially mediated, with the high-afﬁnity hydro- gen oxidizing bacteria (HA-HOB) being widely distributed and active across ecosystems15–21. The magnitude of the global microbial H2 sink is far from constrained and is the largest uncertainty in assessing the indirect global warming potential of H2 11. Hydrol. Earth Syst. Sci., 29, 4983–5003, 2025 https://doi.org/10.5194/hess-29-4983-2025 © Author(s) 2025. This work is distributed under the Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 License. Saudi Rainfall (SaRa): hourly 0.1° gridded rainfall (1979–present) for Saudi Arabia via machine learning fusion of satellite and model data Xuetong Wang1, Raied S. Alharbi3, Oscar M. Baez-Villanueva4, Amy Green5,6, Matthew F. McCabe2, Yoshihide Wada2, Albert I. J. M. Van Dijk7, Muhammad A. Abid8,9, and Hylke E. Beck1,10 1Physical Science and Engineering Division, King Abdullah University of Science and Technology, Thuwal, Saudi Arabia 2Biological and Environmental Science and Engineering Division, King Abdullah University of Science and Technology, Thuwal, Saudi Arabia 3Department of Civil Engineering, College of Engineering, King Saud University, Riyadh, Saudi Arabia 4Hydro-Climate Extremes Lab (H-CEL), Ghent University, Ghent, Belgium 5School of Engineering, Newcastle University, Newcastle upon Tyne, UK 6Tyndall Centre for Climate Change Research, Newcastle University, Newcastle upon Tyne, UK 7Fenner School of Environment & Society, Australian National University, Canberra, ACT, Australia 8Atmospheric, Oceanic and Planetary Physics (AOPP), Department of Physics, University of Oxford, Oxford, UK 9National Centre for Atmospheric Science (NCAS), Leeds, UK 10GloH2O LLC, Princeton, NJ, USA Correspondence: Hylke E. Beck (hylke.beck@gloh2o.org) Received: 21 January 2025 – Discussion started: 3 February 2025 Revised: 11 July 2025 – Accepted: 14 July 2025 – Published: 8 October 2025 Abstract. We introduce Saudi Rainfall (SaRa), a gridded his- torical and near-real-time precipitation (P ) product specif- ically designed for the Arabian Peninsula, one of the most arid, water-stressed, and data-sparse regions on Earth. The product has an hourly 0.1° resolution spanning 1979 to the present and is continuously updated with a latency of less than 2 h. The algorithm underpinning the product in- volves 18 machine learning model stacks trained for dif- ferent combinations of satellite and (re)analysis P products along with several static predictors. As a training target, hourly and daily P observations from gauges in Saudi Ara- bia (n = 113) and globally (n = 14 256) are used. To evalu- ate the performance of SaRa, we carried out the most com- prehensive evaluation of gridded P products in the region to date, using observations from independent gauges (ran- domly excluded from training) in Saudi Arabia as a refer- ence (n = 119). Among the 20 evaluated P products, our new product, SaRa, consistently ranked ﬁrst across all evaluation metrics, including the Kling–Gupta efﬁciency (KGE), cor- relation, bias, peak bias, wet-day bias, and critical success index. Notably, SaRa achieved a median KGE – a summary statistic combining correlation, bias, and variability – of 0.36, while widely used non-gauge-based products such as CHIRP, ERA5, GSMaP V8, and IMERG-L V07 achieved values of −0.07, 0.21, −0.13, and −0.39, respectively. SaRa also out- performed four gauge-based products such as CHIRPS V2, CPC Uniﬁed, IMERG-F V07, and MSWEP V2.8 which had median KGE values of 0.17, −0.03, 0.29, and 0.20, re- spectively. Our new P product – available at https://www. gloh2o.org/sara (last access: 24 September 2025) – addresses a crucial need in the Arabian Peninsula, providing a robust and reliable dataset to support hydrological modeling, wa- ter resource assessments, ﬂood management, and climate re- search.
